(60)

Choosing
M\in\mathcal{M}
which generates
\mathcal{M}
, then
the internal Hom
\mathfrak{M}:=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(M,M)
is an algebra in
\mathcal{C}
.
To see this, set
X=\mathfrak{M}\otimes\mathfrak{M}
and
M_{1}=M_{2}=M
in Eq. (
59
), we obtain

\mathrm{Hom}(\mathfrak{M}\otimes\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{M})\simeq\mathrm{Hom}((\mathfrak{M}\otimes\mathfrak{M})\otimes M,M).

(61)

And set
X=\mathfrak{M}
in Eq. (
60
) and
M_{1}=M_{2}=M
, we obtain a map
\mu_{M}:\mathfrak{M}\otimes M\to M
which corresponds to
\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{M}}:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{M}
.
The multiplication map of the algebra
\mathfrak{M}
is, under the isomorphism (
61
), given by

\mu_{\mathfrak{M}}=\mu_{M}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{M}}\otimes\mu_{M})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{M},M},

(62)

where
\alpha_{\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{M},M}
is the associator mapping
(\mathfrak{M}\otimes\mathfrak{M})\otimes M
to
\mathfrak{M}\otimes(\mathfrak{M}\otimes M)
.
It can be proved that the
\mathcal{C}
-module category
\mathcal{M}
is equivalent to the category of all right
\mathfrak{M}
-modules in
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathcal{M}\simeq\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C})=:\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{M}}
(see
Ostrik 2003
). Here an
\mathfrak{M}
-module
M\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{M}}
is also a left
H
-module, and the
\mathfrak{M}
-action and
H
-action are compatible, thus we can also denote this category as
{{}_{H}}\textsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{M}}
.

The opposite algebra
\mathfrak{M}^{\rm op}
is an
H^{\rm cop}
-module algebra. Taking the smash product of
\mathfrak{M}^{\rm op}
and
H^{\rm cop}
we obtain an
H
-comodule algebra (see Appendix
A
for the definition of smash product)

\mathfrak{A}:=\mathfrak{M}^{\rm op}\#H^{\rm cop}.

(63)

It is showed in
Andruskiewitsch and Mombelli 2007
that

{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}\simeq\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{M}}\simeq\mathcal{M}
.
To summarize, for any
\mathcal{C}
-module category
\mathcal{M}
, we can find a corresponding
H
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
such that this
\mathcal{M}
-module boundary can be regarded as an
\mathfrak{A}
-boundary of the Hopf quantum double phase.
Thus the boundary can be equivalently characterized by an
H
-module algebra
\mathfrak{M}
or an
H
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
.
Note that for a right
\mathfrak{M}
-module
M
, the left
\mathfrak{A}
-action on
M
is given by

(x\#h)\triangleright m=(h\triangleright m)\triangleleft x

(64)

for all
x\in\mathfrak{M}
,
h\in H
and
m\in M
.

Proposition 3
.

For a quantum double phase with bulk Hopf algebra
H
:

1.

For a gapped boundary determined by
\mathfrak{M}
, the boundary excitations are classified by
\mathfrak{M}|\mathfrak{M}
-bimodules in
\mathsf{Rep}(H)
.

2.

For two gapped boundaries determined by
\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathfrak{N}
, the boundary defects from
\mathfrak{M}
-boundary to
\mathfrak{N}
-boundary are classified by
\mathfrak{N}|\mathfrak{M}
-bimodules in
\mathsf{Rep}(H)
.

For the bulk Hopf algebra
H
, the boundary
H
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
and the corresponding boundary
H
-module algebra
\mathfrak{M}
are also correlated by the Yan-Zhu stabilizer
Yan and Zhu 1998
. It is proved
Andruskiewitsch and Mombelli 2007
that,
as
\hat{H}
-comodule algebra (equivalently, as
H
-module algebra)

\mathfrak{M}=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(M,M)\cong\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{A}}(M),

(65)

where
M
is a nonzero
\mathfrak{A}
-module and
\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{A}}(M)
is the Yan-Zhu stabilizer.

Hopf quantum double model

String-net model

Bulk

Hopf algebra
H

UFC
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Rep}(H)

Bulk phase

\mathcal{D}=\mathsf{Rep}(D(H))

\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathsf{Rep}(H)|\mathsf{Rep}(H)}(\mathsf{Rep}(H),\mathsf{Rep}(H))

Boundary

H
-module algebra
\mathfrak{M}

\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{M}}

Boundary phase

\mathcal{B}\simeq{{}_{\mathfrak{M}}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{M}}

\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{M}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{M}})

Boundary defect

{{}_{\mathfrak{N}}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{M}}

\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{M}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{N}})

Table 2:
The dictionary between
H
-module algebra description of Hopf quantum double boundary and string-net boundary.

We have established a complete theory about the gapped boundary of the Hopf quantum double phase.
However, it is possible that two different
H
-comodule algebras give the same topological boundary
\mathcal{M}
.
This has been reflected in the above discussion of constructing the
H
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
from a given
\mathcal{C}
-module
\mathcal{M}
: by choosing different non-zero simple objects
M\in\mathcal{M}
, we obtain different
H
-module algebras
\mathfrak{M}=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(M,M)
, and they all give the same
\mathcal{C}
-module category
\mathcal{M}
.
In Hopf algebraic language, this is captured by the equivariant Morita equivalence of
H
-comodule algebras (see Appendix
A
for a detailed discussion).
If two
H
-comodule algebras
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
are equivariantly Morita equivalent, then
{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}\simeq{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod}
,
viz.
, they give the same topological boundary.
For
H
-module algebra description, the same result holds.

Proposition 4
.

Two boundary models determined by
H
-comodule algebras
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
(resp.
H
-module algebras
\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathfrak{N}
) are topologically equivalent if and only if
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
(resp.
\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathfrak{N}
) are equivariantly Morita equivalent.

Notice that from Yan-Zhu stabilizer and Eq. (
63
), we will obtain an
H
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
. This
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
may be different from
\mathfrak{A}
, but they are Morita equivalent, thus they give the same gapped boundary.

To summarize, a gapped boundary for Hopf quantum double phase is determined by Morita equivalent class of
H
-comodule algebras or
H
-module algebras.
The module category of the corresponding boundary is given by the module category over these algebras. The topological excitations are classified by bimodules over these algebras.

Example 6
.

Consider the smooth and rough boundaries in Examples
3
and
4
.
For smooth boundary,
\mathfrak{M}_{s}=\mathbb{C}
, and the boundary module category is
\mathcal{M}_{s}\simeq\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathsf{Rep}(H))\simeq\mathsf{Rep}(H)
; for rough boundary,
\mathfrak{M}=\hat{H}
, and the boundary module is
\mathcal{M}_{r}\simeq\mathsf{Mod}_{\hat{H}}(\mathsf{Rep}(H))\simeq\mathsf{Vect}
. This matches well with the result of lattice construction.

Example 7
.

It is worth discussing how to recover the result of boundary theory for finite group quantum double
H=\mathbb{C}[G]
in our framework.
From Refs.
Bombin and Martin-Delgado 2008
;
Beigi et al. 2011
;
Kitaev and Kong 2012
;
Cong et al. 2017
;
Etingof and Ostrik 2004
;
Ostrik 2003
;
Andruskiewitsch and Mombelli 2007
;
Natale 2017
, the boundary is described by a pair
(N,\sigma)
where
N\leq G
is a subgroup up to conjugacy and
\sigma\in H^{2}(N,\mathbb{C}^{\times})
is a
2
-cocycle.
The twisted group algebra
\mathbb{C}_{\sigma}[N]
is a
\mathbb{C}[G]
-comodule algebra via
\beta_{\mathbb{C}_{\sigma}[N]}(x)=x\otimes x
for all
x\in N
.
The bulk UFC is
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Rep}(G)
and the boundary is the
\mathsf{Rep}(G)
-module category
\mathcal{M}(N,\sigma)=\mathsf{Rep}(\tilde{N})
, where
\tilde{N}
is the extension of
N
under
\sigma
.
The
H
-comodule algebra for this boundary is
\mathfrak{A}=\mathbb{C}_{\sigma}[N]
.
The
H
-module algebra for this boundary is
\mathfrak{M}=\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathbb{C}_{\sigma}[N]}(M)=\mathbb{C}[G]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[N]}\operatorname{End}(M)
for some
\mathbb{C}_{\sigma}[N]
-module
M
.

3.2.2
Mapping quantum double boundary to string-net boundary

We have proved the topological nature of our Hopf quantum double boundary from an algebraic theory perspective. Let us now prove it from the perspective of lattice Hamiltonian.
We will show how to map a quantum double boundary to a string-net boundary (which realizes an extended Turaev-Viro TQFT, thus is topological), and also discuss, how a string-net boundary can be mapped to a quantum double boundary.

We first introduce the Fourier transformation for a general Hopf algebra
H
with Haar integral
h_{H}
:

|\nu;a,b\rangle=|[\nu]_{ab}\rangle=\sqrt{\frac{\dim\nu}{\dim H}}\sum_{(h_{H})}D^{\nu}(h_{H}^{(1)})_{ab}h_{H}^{(2)},

(66)

where
D^{\nu}
is a fixed matrix representation of
\nu\in\operatorname{Irr}(H)
, and
a,b=1,\cdots,\dim\nu
. Here
\operatorname{Irr}(H)
is the set of all equivalence classes of irreducible representations of
H
.
This orthonormal basis is usually called the fusion basis. We have (Peter–Weyl theorem, a.k.a., Artin-Wedderburn theorem)

H\cong\sum_{\nu\in\operatorname{Irr}(H)}V_{\nu}\otimes V_{\nu^{\vee}},

(67)

and thus
\dim H=\sum_{\nu\in\operatorname{Irr}(H)}(\dim\nu)^{2}
. So they form a complete basis.
For the convenience of later discussion, we assume that the Hopf quantum double model is built on a trivalent lattice with boundary
6
6

6

This is just for convenience for discussion; for any other lattice, the generalization is straightforward.
.
The general approach to mapping the quantum double model on a closed surface to an extended string-net model is presented in
Buerschaper and Aguado 2009
;
Buerschaper et al. 2013b
;
Hu et al. 2018a
. A mathematical proof that shows Hopf quantum double model gives the same topological invariant as Turaev-Viro TQFT is presented in
Balsam and Kirillov 2012
.
The input data of an extended string-net model is
(\mathcal{C},\omega_{\mathcal{C}})_{\rm SN}
with
\mathcal{C}
a UFC and
\omega_{\mathcal{C}}
a fiber functor,
viz.
, a monoidal functor
\omega_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathsf{Vect}
.
The input data of a Hopf quantum double model is a Hopf algebra
(H)_{\rm QD}
. By setting
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Rep}(H)
, the Hopf quantum double model can be mapped into a string-net model.
Using Tannaka–Krein reconstruction
Etingof et al. 2016
, and by setting
H=\operatorname{End}(\omega_{\mathcal{C}})
(the natural endomorphism of
\omega_{\mathcal{C}}
which has a canonical Hopf algebra structure), the extended string-net model can be mapped into a Hopf quantum double model.

For our case, we only need to consider how to map the boundary of the Hopf quantum double model to the extended string-net boundary.
Recall that extended string-net boundary is determined by a
\mathcal{C}
-module category
\mathcal{M}
together with an additive functor
\omega_{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathsf{Vect}
.
They satisfy
7
7

7

Notice that two tensor products are different. The upper one is for
\mathcal{C}
-module structure over
\mathcal{M}
, while the lower one is for
\mathsf{Vect}
-module structure over
\mathsf{Vect}
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(68)

From the previous discussion of algebraic theory we know that
\mathcal{M}={{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}
. We can choose
\omega_{\mathcal{M}}
as a forgetful functor. We will establish the following correspondence:

Proposition 5
.

The Hopf quantum double model with boundary
(H,\mathfrak{A})_{\rm QD}
is equivalent to the extended string-net model with boundary
(\mathcal{C},\omega_{\mathcal{C}},\mathcal{M},\omega_{\mathcal{M}})_{\rm SN}
:

1.

A Hopf quantum double model can be mapped to an extended string-net model by setting: bulk
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Rep}(H)
, and boundary
\mathcal{M}={{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}
.

2.

An extended string-net model with boundary
(\mathcal{C},\omega_{\mathcal{C}},\mathcal{M},\omega_{\mathcal{M}})
can be mapped to a Hopf quantum double model by setting:
H=\operatorname{End}(\omega_{\mathcal{C}})
and
\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{M}^{\rm op}\#H^{\rm cop}
with
\mathfrak{M}=\underline{\rm Hom}(M,M)
for some nonzero
M\in\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

A rigorous proof for the general
H
-comodule algebra is complicated and lengthy, it will be given in our forthcoming work
Jia and Tan
. Here we only prove that the commutative diagram in Eq. (
68
)
holds when we set
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Rep}(H)
and
\mathcal{M}={{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}
.
When acting on objects
X\in\mathsf{Rep}(H)
and
M\in{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}
, for the upper right path, the tensor product is given by
X\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}M
, then acting
\omega_{{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}}
on it we obtain
\omega_{{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\otimes(X,M)=X\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}M
.
For the lower left path, it is clear that we still obtain
\otimes\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\omega_{\mathcal{C}}\times\omega_{{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}})(X,M)=X\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}M
.
Now consider the morphisms
(f,g):X\times M\to Y\times N
, it is clear that
\otimes\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\omega_{\mathcal{C}}\times\omega_{{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}})(f,g)=\omega_{{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathsf{Mod}}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\otimes(f,g)
.

The following proof is for the special case that
\mathfrak{A}=K\leq H
, where all data are clear for both the extended string-net model and for the Hopf quantum double model.

1.
Suppose that the boundary is characterized by an
H
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}=K\leq H
. For a boundary vertex
v_{b}
connecting two boundary edges
e_{1},e_{2}
and a bulk edge
e_{3}
, the boundary edge space is
\mathcal{H}_{e_{1}}=\mathcal{H}_{e_{2}}=\mathfrak{A}
and the bulk edge space is
\mathcal{H}_{e_{3}}=H
. Thus
\mathcal{H}(v_{b})=H\otimes\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}
.
By taking the Fourier transform for
H
and
\mathfrak{A}
, we obtain

(69)

The coefficient can be obtained by taking the inner product of each, e.g.,
\langle\nu;a,b|g\rangle=\sqrt{\frac{\dim\nu}{\dim H}}\sum_{(h_{H})}D^{\nu}(h_{H}^{(1)})_{ab}^{*}\langle h_{H}^{(2)}|g\rangle

8
8

8

Recall that the inner product of
H
is given by
\langle h|g\rangle=\varphi_{\hat{H}}(h^{*}g)
.

To obtain the Fourier transform of the vertex operator
A_{v_{b}}^{K}
,
it is sufficient to consider the
K
action over the fusion basis of
H
and
K
. Taking
\mathfrak{A}=K
fusion basis as an example, it is easy to verify that

L^{k}_{+}|[\nu]_{ab}\rangle=\sum_{c}D^{\nu}(S(k))_{ac}|[\nu]_{cb}\rangle,\quad L_{-}^{k}|[\nu]_{ab}\rangle=\sum_{c}D^{\nu}(k)_{cb}|[\nu]_{ac}\rangle,

(70)

where the second one can be derived from the first one by using Eq. (
174
).
Then we see that

A^{K}_{v_{b}}|[\nu_{1}]_{a_{1}b_{1}},[\nu_{2}]_{a_{2}b_{2}},[\mu_{3}]_{a_{3}b_{3}}\rangle=\sum_{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}}[W^{\nu_{2}}_{\mu_{3}\nu_{1}}]_{\{a_{1},b_{2},a_{3}\},\{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\}}|[\nu_{1}]_{c_{1}b_{1}},[\nu_{2}]_{a_{2}c_{2}},[\mu_{3}]_{c_{3}b_{3}}\rangle,

(71)

where the
3j
-symbol

[W^{\nu_{2}}_{\mu_{3}\nu_{1}}]_{\{a_{1},b_{2},a_{3}\},\{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\}}=\sum_{(h_{K})}D^{\nu_{1}}(S(h_{K}^{(1)}))_{a_{1},c_{1}}\otimes D^{\nu_{2}}(h_{K}^{(2)})_{c_{2},b_{2}}\otimes D^{\mu_{2}}(S(h_{K}^{(3)}))_{a_{3},c_{3}}

(72)

is the projector on the space
\mathrm{Hom}(\mu_{3}\otimes\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
.
This coincides with the string-net boundary vertex operator.

In the same spirit, for boundary face
f_{b}
(with one boundary edge
e_{1}
and three bulk edges
e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}
), we need to consider the
\hat{H}
action
T^{\varphi}_{\pm}
on fusion bases of
H
and
K
. It is clear that for
H
(and similar for
K
)

\displaystyle T^{\varphi}_{+}|[\nu]_{ab}

\displaystyle\rangle=\sqrt{\frac{\dim\nu}{\dim H}}\sum_{(h_{H})}D^{\nu}(h_{H}^{(1)})_{ab}\varphi(h_{H}^{(3)})|h_{H}^{(2)}\rangle,

(73)

\displaystyle T^{\varphi}_{-}|[\nu]_{ab}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\dim\nu}{\dim H}}\sum_{(h_{H})}D^{\nu}(h_{H}^{(1)})_{ab}\varphi(S(h_{H}^{(2)}))|h_{H}^{(3)}\rangle.

(74)

Notice that we have

\varphi_{\hat{H}}=\sum_{\nu\in\operatorname{Irr}(H)}\frac{\dim\nu}{\dim H}\chi_{\nu},

(75)

where
\chi_{\nu}
is the character of
\nu
. The boundary phase operator can be written as

B_{f_{b}}=\sum_{\nu}\frac{\dim\nu}{\dim H}B_{f_{b}}^{\nu},

(76)

where

B_{f_{b}}^{\nu}=B_{f_{b}}^{\chi_{\nu}}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{4}\operatorname{ev}_{i}\left(\bigoplus_{\mu_{i},\zeta_{i}}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{4}\sqrt{\dim\mu_{i}\dim\zeta_{i}}W_{\mu_{i},\zeta_{i},\nu_{i}}\right).

(77)

Suppose all edges surrounds
f_{b}
counterclockwise, then using Eqs. (
73
) and (
74
) one has

\displaystyle\langle\{[\nu_{i}]_{a_{i}b_{i}}\}|B_{f_{b}}^{\chi_{\nu}}|\{[\nu^{\prime}_{j}]_{a^{\prime}_{j}b^{\prime}_{j}}\}\rangle

(78)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(\chi_{\nu}),(h_{K,1}),(h_{H,2,3,4})}\sqrt{\frac{\dim\nu^{\prime}_{1}}{\dim K}}D^{\nu^{\prime}_{1}}(h_{K,1}^{(1)})_{a^{\prime}_{1}b^{\prime}_{1}}\chi_{\nu}^{(1)}(h_{K,1}^{(3)})\langle[\nu_{1}]_{a_{1}b_{1}}|h_{K,1}^{(2)}\rangle

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=2}^{4}\sqrt{\frac{\dim\nu^{\prime}_{j}}{\dim H}}D^{\nu^{\prime}_{j}}(h_{H,j}^{(1)})_{a^{\prime}_{j}b^{\prime}_{j}}\chi_{\nu}^{(j)}(h_{H,j}^{(3)})\langle[\nu_{j}]_{a_{j}b_{j}}|h^{(2)}_{H,j}\rangle.

It is easy to verify that this matches well with the string-net face operator.

and

with
K\leq H
.

\lxSVG@closescope {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.52328pt}{-13.53369pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.75012pt}{10.20036pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{1.07913pt}{-1.40277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{aligned}\quad\quad\quad\begin{aligned} \mathcal{H}_{e}&=\bigoplus_{i\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{C})}\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(i)\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(i)^{\vee}\cong H,\\
\mathcal{H}_{e_{b}}&=\bigoplus_{x\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{M})}\omega_{\mathcal{M}}(x)\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{M}}(x)^{\vee}\cong K,\end{aligned}

(79)

where
\omega_{\mathcal{C}}
and
\omega_{\mathcal{M}}
are forgetful functors of the representation.
For a trivalent boundary vertex
v_{b}
, the corresponding space is
\mathcal{H}_{v_{b}}=\mathcal{H}_{e}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{e_{b}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{e_{b}}
.
As we have shown before, since
K
is a left
H
-comodule algebra, the tensor product
i\otimes x
for representations
i\in\operatorname{Irr}(H)
and
x\in\operatorname{Irr}(K)
is a representation of
K
, the fusion rule is

i\otimes x=\bigoplus_{y\in\operatorname{Irr}(K)}N_{i,x}^{y}y.

(80)

Since
\omega_{\mathcal{M}}
preserves that tensor product (Eq. (
68
)) and direct sum (this holds by definition), we have

\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(i)\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{M}}(x)\cong\bigoplus_{y\in\operatorname{Irr}(K)}N_{i,x}^{y}\omega_{\mathcal{M}}(y).

(81)

The boundary vertex operator is defined as the projection that characterizes the above isomorphism
Q_{v_{b}}^{i,x;y}:\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(i)\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{M}}(x)\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{M}}(y)\to\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(i)\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{M}}(x)\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{M}}(y)
. Namely, if
N_{i,x}^{y}\neq 0
,
Q_{v_{b}}^{i,x;y}=1
, otherwise,
Q_{v_{b}}^{i,x;y}=0
. The boundary vertex operator is thus given by

Q_{v_{b}}:=\bigoplus_{i\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{C}),\,x,y\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{M})}Q_{v_{b}}^{i,x;y}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(i)^{\vee}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\omega_{\mathcal{M}}(x)^{\vee}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\omega_{\mathcal{M}}(y)^{\vee}}.

(82)

To show that
Q_{v_{b}}
coincides with the quantum double boundary vertex operator
A_{v_{b}}
, we only need to use the fact that the
3j
-symbol we defined in Eq. (
72
) coincides with
Q_{v_{b}}^{i,x;y}
.

We will employ the definition of the face operator provided in Ref.
Buerschaper et al. 2013b
. Through our analysis, we will demonstrate that, in this particular instance, the face operator aligns with the quantum double face operator as per its definition.

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-23.68481pt}{-2.32541pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.72256pt}{-23.21446pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.76042pt}{16.44463pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{aligned}\quad\quad B_{f}:\mathcal{H}_{e}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{e}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{e}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{e_{b}}\to\mathcal{H}_{e}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{e}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{e}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{e_{b}}.

(83)

If we denote the domain and codomain face spaces of
B_{f}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{f_{b}}=

\displaystyle(\oplus_{i\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{C})}(\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(i)\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(i)^{\vee}))\otimes(\oplus_{j\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{C})}(\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(j)\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(j)^{\vee}))

(84)

\displaystyle\otimes(\oplus_{k\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{C})}(\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(k)\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(k)^{\vee}))\otimes(\oplus_{x\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{M})}(\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(x)\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(x)^{\vee}))

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{f_{b}}=

\displaystyle(\oplus_{i^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{C})}(\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(i^{\prime})\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(i^{\prime})^{\vee}))\otimes(\oplus_{j^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{C})}(\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(j^{\prime})\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(j^{\prime})^{\vee}))

(85)

\displaystyle\otimes(\oplus_{k^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{C})}(\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(k^{\prime})\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(k^{\prime})^{\vee}))\otimes(\oplus_{x^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{M})}(\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(x^{\prime})\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(x^{\prime})^{\vee})),

then the face operator can be regarded as an element in

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{f_{b}}\otimes(\mathcal{H}_{f_{b}}^{\prime})^{\vee}=\oplus_{i,j,k,x}\oplus_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k^{\prime},x^{\prime}}

\displaystyle((\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(i)\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(i)^{\vee})\otimes(\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(i^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(i^{\prime})))

(86)

\displaystyle\otimes

\displaystyle((\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(j)\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(j)^{\vee})\otimes(\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(j^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(j^{\prime})))

\displaystyle\otimes

\displaystyle((\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(k)\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(k)^{\vee})\otimes(\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(k^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(k^{\prime})))

\displaystyle\otimes

\displaystyle((\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(x)\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(x)^{\vee})\otimes(\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(x^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes\omega_{\mathcal{C}}(x^{\prime}))).

Fix the
\nu\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{C})
, we defined

P^{i,i^{\prime\vee},\nu}
as the projection of the tensor product of three representations into the vacuum,
i\otimes i^{\prime\vee}\otimes\nu\to\mathds{1}
.
Notice that for the boundary edge, the projection to vacuum is to the vacuum of
\mathds{1}_{K}\in\mathsf{Rep}(K)
, and for the bulk edge, it is the projection to vacuum
\mathds{1}_{H}
.
For each edge, we assign the same
\nu
, and label the edges around the faces as
s=1,\cdots,4
, then the
\nu
-component of string-net face operator is defined as

B_{f_{b}}^{\nu}=\bigotimes_{s=1}^{4}\operatorname{ev}_{s}\left(\bigoplus_{\mu_{s},\zeta_{s}}\bigotimes_{s=1}^{4}\sqrt{\dim\mu_{s}\dim\zeta_{s}}P^{\mu_{s},\zeta^{\vee}_{s},\nu}\right).

(87)

We see that it is of the same form as Eq. (
77
).
The boundary face operator

B_{f_{b}}=\sum_{\nu}\frac{\dim\nu}{\dim H}B_{f_{b}}^{\nu},

(88)

thus coincides with the face operator of that of the quantum double model, as expected.
∎

3.3
Anyon condensation via ribbon operator

In this section, we will delve into the lattice construction of local operator algebra, as well as ribbon operators and their application in realizing anyon condensation and boundary-bulk duality.

From the perspective of anyon condensation theory, the boundary vacuum anyon can be viewed as a composite of bulk anyons, represented by the equation
\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{B}}=\mathds{1}\oplus X_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus X_{n}
.
In the lattice realization, the boundary vacuum anyon sector is the space of states that are invariant under all boundary local stabilizers, i.e.,

V_{\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}=\{|\psi\rangle:A_{v_{b}}|\psi\rangle=B_{f_{b}}|\psi\rangle=|\psi\rangle,\forall~v_{b},f_{b}\}.

(89)

The anyon condensation process occurs when a bulk anyon moves near the boundary and becomes a boundary anyon.
Since the bulk phase is described by a UMTC
\mathcal{D}
and the boundary phase is described by a UFC
\mathcal{B}
, the condensation is characterized mathematically by a monoidal functor
\mathbf{Cond}:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{B}
(roughly speaking, this is a functor that preserves the fusion structure).
The bulk anyons that become boundary vacuum anyon are called condensed.
Our goal is to construct ribbon operators that can facilitate the anyon condensation process and explore the boundary-bulk duality.

Before we start, let us introduce some notions that we will use:
(i) the bulk-to-boundary ribbon
\rho_{\downarrow}
is a directed ribbon that starts at some bulk site
s=\partial_{0}\rho
and ends at some boundary site
s_{b}=\partial_{1}\rho
;
(ii) the boundary-to-bulk ribbon
\rho_{\uparrow}
starts at some boundary site and ends at some bulk site;
(iii) the boundary ribbon
\rho_{b}
is a ribbon that starts and terminates on boundary sites
s_{b}^{0}=\partial_{0}\rho_{b}
and
s_{b}^{1}=\partial_{1}\rho_{b}
. These ribbons can also be called type-A or type-B according to the same convention as we have adopted in the bulk case (see Sec.
2.2
).

Topological excitations are characterized as point-like excitations in the lattice model, and are represented by the local operator algebra. Specifically, for a given bulk site
s
, the vertex operator
A^{g}(s)
and
B^{\psi}(s)
generate an algebra
\mathcal{D}(s)
, which is isomorphic to the quantum double of the input Hopf algebra. Topological excitations correspond to irreducible representations of these local operator algebras.
The bulk ribbon operator is constructed to generate an algebra
\mathcal{A}
, which is isomorphic to the dual algebra of
\mathcal{D}(s)
. Local stabilizers at the internal sites of the ribbon commute with the ribbon operator, but at the two ends, they do not commute, and their commutators correspond to topological excitations when the ribbon operators act on the ground state.
For a gapped boundary, a similar picture exists. Boundary excitations correspond to representations of the boundary local operator algebra, and the bulk-to-boundary ribbon operator algebra is dual to the boundary local operator algebra.
In the previous section, we demonstrated that the gapped boundaries of a bulk Hopf algebra
H
are classified by
H
-indecomposable left (or right)
H
-comodule algebras
\mathfrak{A}
. We will now determine the algebraic structure of the boundary local operator algebra and bulk-to-boundary ribbon operators.

\mathcal{C}
-module

Boundary phase

Boundary local algebra

Left
H
-comodule

{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\textsf{Mod}

{{}^{H}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\textsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{A}}

(\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}^{\rm op})\star\hat{H}

Right
H
-comodule

\textsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{A}}

{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\textsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{A}}^{H}

(\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}^{\rm op})\star\hat{H}

Table 3:
The boundary local operator algebras, see Eq. (
47
) for the definition of the crossed product
(\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}^{\rm op})\star\hat{H}

for a right
H
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
. The left comodule algebra case can be defined in a similar way. Notice that, by employing a slight abuse of notation, both cases are denoted as
(\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}^{\rm op})\star\hat{H}
.

Proposition 6
.

Consider a gapped boundary determined by an
H
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
:

1.

The boundary local operator algebra is fusion-categorical
Morita equivalent to
\mathcal{D}(s_{b})\simeq(\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}^{\rm op})\star\hat{H}
.
9
9

9

In this paper, we use
A\cong B
to denote the isomorphism between algebraic objects, and use
A\simeq B
to denote the Morita equivalence between them.
See Eq. (
47
) for the definition of
(\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}^{\rm op})\star\hat{H}
, also see Table
3
for a summary.

2.

The bulk-to-boundary ribbon operator algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\rho_{\downarrow}}
is the dual of the boundary local operator algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\rho_{\downarrow}}\simeq\mathcal{D}(s_{b})^{\vee}
. Or equivalently, the dual of
the bulk-to-boundary ribbon operator algebra is fusion-categorical Morita equivalent to the boundary local operator algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\rho_{\downarrow}}^{\vee}\simeq(\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}^{\rm op})\star\hat{H}
.

Remark 3.2
.

Before we prove the main assertions, it is important to recall that two algebras are considered fusion-categorical Morita equivalent if and only if their respective representation categories are equivalent as fusion categories.
In the case of a gapped boundary, the local operator algebra is unique up to fusion-categorical Morita equivalence. The only requirement is that the representation category of the local operator algebra is equivalent to the boundary topological phase as UFC. As a result, the category of
\mathcal{D}(s_{b})
-modules is equivalent to either
\mathcal{B}={{}_{\mathfrak{A}}^{H}}\textsf{Mod}{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}
or
\mathcal{B}={{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\textsf{Mod}{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}^{H}}
, depending on the choice of boundary directions (with the bulk either on the left- or right-hand side of the boundary), see Table
1
for a summary.
The bulk-to-boundary ribbon operator algebra is dual to the boundary local operator algebra.
At the boundary end, the ribbon operator algebra generates all the boundary excitations.
Because the local operator algebra is not unique, it follows that the ribbon operator algebra is also not unique. This is reflected in the fact that there exist many different lattice constructions that realize the same phase.

Proof of Proposition
6
.

We only need to prove that 1 and 2 are statements about the requirement that bulk-to-boundary ribbon operator algebra must satisfy.

Without loss of generality, let us consider the right-
H
comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
, and suppose that the boundary phase is given by
\mathcal{B}={{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\textsf{Mod}{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}^{H}}
.
A module
M\in\mathcal{B}
is an
\mathfrak{A}|\mathfrak{A}
-bimodule with a right
H
-comodule structure with the coaction

\beta:M\to M\otimes H,

(90)

such that the coaction is an
\mathfrak{A}|\mathfrak{A}
-bimodule map (the
\mathfrak{A}|\mathfrak{A}
-bimodule structure of
M\otimes H
is given by
a\cdot(m\otimes h)\cdot b=\sum(a^{[0]}\cdot m\cdot b^{[0]})\otimes(a^{[1]}\cdot h\cdot b^{[1]})
).
This means that we have

\sum_{[a\cdot m\cdot b]}(a\cdot m\cdot b)^{[0]}\otimes(a\cdot m\cdot b)^{[1]}=\sum_{[a],[m],[b]}a^{[0]}\cdot m^{[0]}\cdot b^{[0]}\otimes a^{[1]}\cdot m^{[1]}\cdot b^{[1]}.

(91)

A left
\hat{H}
-module structure of
M
can be defined as

\varphi\rightharpoonup m=\sum_{[m]}m^{[0]}\varphi(m^{[1]}).

(92)

The condition of Eq. (
91
) can be expressed in the left
\hat{H}
-action as

\varphi\rightharpoonup(a\cdot m\cdot b)=\sum_{[a],[m],[b]}a^{[0]}\cdot m^{[0]}\cdot b^{[0]}\varphi(a^{[1]}\cdot m^{[1]}\cdot b^{[1]}).

(93)

Now a direct calculation gives (
\star
is just tensor product here)

((c\otimes d)\star\psi)\cdot((a\otimes b)\star\varphi)\cdot m=\sum_{[a],[m],[b]}(ca^{[0]}m^{[0]}b^{[0]}d)\star\psi(a^{[1]}m^{[1]}b^{[1]})\varphi(m^{[2]}),

(94)

where
a,b,c,d\in\mathfrak{A}
and
\psi,\varphi\in\hat{H}
.
This implies that

((a\otimes b)\star\varphi)\cdot((c\otimes d)\star\psi)=\sum(ac^{[0]}\otimes d^{[0]}b)\star\varphi(c^{[1]}\bullet d^{[1]})\psi,

(95)

which coincides with Eq. (
47
).
This means that
M\in\mathcal{B}
implies that
M\in{{}_{(\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}^{\rm op})\star\hat{H}}}\textsf{Mod}
.
The proof of the converse direction can be showed similarly since the left
\hat{H}
-action and the right
H
-coaction correspond to each other in a canonical way.
∎

In Proposition
1
, we demonstrated that the boundary local vertex and face operators generate the boundary local algebra
\mathcal{D}(s_{b})
. This result supports the claim that the lattice model we have constructed realizes the boundary phase of
\mathcal{B}={{}_{\mathfrak{A}}}\textsf{Mod}{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}^{H}}
.
We would like to note that the above proposition holds not only when the input
\mathfrak{A}
for the boundary is a right
H
-comodule algebra, but also when
\mathfrak{A}
is a left
H
-comodule algebra. This is summarized in Table
3
.
When
\mathfrak{A}
is a faithfully flat
H
-Galois extension, the local operator algebra can be showed to be fusion-categorically equivalent to either
\mathfrak{A}\Box_{H}\bar{\mathfrak{A}}
or
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}\Box_{H}\mathfrak{A}

Caenepeel et al. 2007
. For further details, please refer to Eq. (
211
) and the accompanying discussions. This equivalence enables the construction of a gapped boundary model via a generalized quantum double construction, as outlined in Appendix
D
. Using this approach can simplify the lattice realization of ribbon operators and other related concepts.

The case where
\mathfrak{A}=K\leq H
is of interest in its own right, we can provide a more explicit lattice construction of ribbon operators and anyon condensation.
By discussion in Sec.
3.2
, we have known that for the boundary model determined by an
H
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}=K\leq H
, the boundary topological excitations are classified by
H
-covariant
\mathfrak{A}|\mathfrak{A}
-bimodules. Or equivalently, they are also classified by
\mathfrak{M}|\mathfrak{M}
-bimodules in
\mathsf{Rep}(H)
, where
\mathfrak{M}\cong\mathrm{Stab}_{K}(M)
is the Yan-Zhu stabilizer for some nonzero
K
-module
M
and is an
H
-module algebra. One can take
M=\mathbb{C}
the trivial
K
-module. In this case, it is showed
(
Andruskiewitsch and Mombelli 2007
, Example 2.19)
that
\mathfrak{M}\cong\mathrm{Stab}_{K}(\mathbb{C})\cong\underline{K}^{\vee}
as right
\hat{H}
-comodule algebras with
\underline{K}:=H/S^{-1}(K^{+})H
and
K^{+}:=\ker(\varepsilon)\cap K
the augmentation ideal of
K
.
The
\hat{H}
-comodule algebra structure will canonically induces an
H
-module algebra structure on
\underline{K}^{\vee}
.
The bulk-to-boundary ribbon operators are operators supported on bulk-to-boundary ribbons and commute with all stabilizer operators in the Hamiltonian except at the two end sites of the ribbons. These operators form an algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\rho_{\downarrow}}
. Proposition
6
has provided one way to identify this algebra up to a fusion-categorical Morita equivalence.

3.3.1
Local operator algebra

In this subsection, we will determine the local operator algebra
\mathcal{D}(s_{b})
over the boundary site
s_{b}
in the case that
\mathfrak{A}=K
is a Hopf subalgebra of
H
.

Since boundary topological excitations are given as the monoidal category
{{}^{H}_{K}}\mathsf{Mod}_{K}
, identifying the local operator algebra amounts to the reconstruction problem: if there is some kind of algebraic object whose representation (modules or comodules) category is equivalent to
{{}^{H}_{K}}\mathsf{Mod}_{K}
. A more general case has been studied in
Schauenburg 2002a
;
Schauenburg 2002b
which applies to our case. Note that
K\leq H
are finite-dimensional complex Hopf algebras, hence
H
is cocleft as a left
K
-module coalgebra (see Refs.
Schauenburg 2002a
;
Schauenburg 2002b
and references therein for related definition). Denote by
\pi:H\to K
the convolution invertible cocleaving map which is unital and counital. Denote
\bar{M}:=M/K^{+}M
for an
H
-covariant
K|K
-bimodule
M
. It can be showed that
\overline{M\otimes_{K}N}\cong\bar{M}\otimes\bar{N}
. Let
j:\bar{M}\to M
be the map
j(\bar{m})=\sum_{(m)}\pi^{-1}(m^{[-1]})m^{[0]}
. One has an isomorphism
M\cong K\otimes\bar{M},m\mapsto\sum_{(m)}\pi(m^{[-1]})\otimes\overline{{m^{[0]}}}
with inverse
x\otimes\bar{m}\mapsto xj(\bar{m})
. In particular,
H\cong K\otimes(H/K^{+}H)
as left
K
-modules and right
H/K^{+}H
-comodules, thus
\pi:H\to K
and
j:H/K^{+}H\to H
can be identified with
\pi(x\otimes\bar{h})=\varepsilon(h)x
and
j(\bar{h})=1_{K}\otimes\bar{h}
respectively for
x\in K,\bar{h}\in H/K^{+}H
.

Define the functor
\omega:{{}^{H}_{K}}\mathsf{Mod}_{K}\to\mathsf{Vect}
by
\omega(M)=\bar{M}=M/K^{+}M
. It is a tensor functor with the factorial isomorphisms
\xi_{M,N}:\bar{M}\otimes\bar{N}\to\overline{M\otimes_{K}N}
given by
\xi(\bar{m}\otimes\bar{n})=\overline{m\otimes j(\bar{n})}
, with
\xi_{M,N}^{-1}(\overline{m\otimes n})=\sum_{(n)}\overline{m\pi(n^{[-1]})}\otimes\overline{n^{[0]}}
.
Consider the category
{{}^{H/K^{+}H}}\mathsf{Mod}_{K}
of
H/K^{+}H
-covariant right
K
-modules, that is, right
K
-modules such that the
H/K^{+}H
-coactions are morphisms of right
K
-modules. It is showed
Schauenburg 2002b
by Schneider’s structure theorem that there is a category equivalence
\hat{\omega}:{{}^{H}_{K}}\mathsf{Mod}_{K}\simeq{{}^{H/K^{+}H}}\mathsf{Mod}_{K},M\mapsto\bar{M}
, whose inverse is
N\mapsto H\Box_{H/K^{+}H}N
. In fact, the
H/K^{+}H
-comodule structure over
\bar{M}
is induced by the
H
-comodule structure over
M
, and
\bar{m}x=\overline{mx}
for
m\in M,x\in K
gives the right
K
-action; the left
H
-comodule and
K
-module structures over
H\Box_{H/K^{+}H}N
are induced by the left tensor factor, and
(\sum h_{i}\otimes n_{i})x=\sum h_{i}x^{(1)}\otimes n_{i}x^{(2)}
for
x\in K,\sum h_{i}\otimes n_{i}\in H\Box_{H/K^{+}H}N
. There is a unique monoidal category structure over
{{}^{H/K^{+}H}}\mathsf{Mod}_{K}
making
\hat{\omega}
a monoidal equivalence such that
\omega
factors over
\hat{\omega}
(see
(
Schauenburg 2002b
, Theorem 3.3.5)
):

\hbox to215.32pt{\vbox to52.15pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 107.65706pt\lower-25.78172pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{31.37794pt}{-4.77005pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The next step is to identify an algebraic object
\tilde{H}
such that
{{}^{H/K^{+}H}}\mathsf{Mod}_{K}\simeq{{}^{\tilde{H}}}\mathsf{Mod}
. It turns out that
\tilde{H}=(H/K^{+}H)\rtimes\hat{K}
is a coquasi-Hopf algebra which is a crossed coproduct built on
(H/K^{+}H)\otimes\hat{K}
with structures given below (cf.
(
Schauenburg 2002a
, Sec. 4.2)
). First define
\rightharpoonup\,:\hat{K}\otimes(H/K^{+}H)\to H/K^{+}H
,
\leftharpoonup\,:\hat{K}\otimes(H/K^{+}H)\to\hat{K}
by

\varphi\rightharpoonup\bar{h}=\hat{\varepsilon}(\varphi)\bar{h},\quad(\varphi\leftharpoonup\bar{h})(x)=\varepsilon(h)\varphi(x),

and
\rho:H/K^{+}H\to\hat{K}\otimes(H/K^{+}H),~\rho(\bar{h}):=\sum\rho(\bar{h})^{[-1]}\otimes\rho(\bar{h})^{[0]}
by

\sum\rho(\bar{h})^{[-1]}(x)\rho(\bar{h})^{[0]}=\overline{j(\bar{h})x},\quad\text{for~all}~x\in K.

Using this definition, one computes that

\rho(\bar{h})=\sum_{k}\sum_{(k)}\hat{k}(S(k^{(3)})\bullet k^{(1)})\otimes\overline{hk^{(2)}},

where
\{k\}
is an orthogonal basis of
K
with
\{\hat{k}\}
its dual basis. By this preparation,
(H/K^{+}H)\rtimes\hat{K}
becomes a coquasi-bialgebra with
\varepsilon(\bar{h}\otimes\varphi)=\varepsilon_{H}(h)\varphi(1_{K})
,
1=\overline{1_{H}}\otimes\varepsilon_{K}
,

\displaystyle(\bar{h}\otimes\varphi)(\bar{g}\otimes

\displaystyle\psi)=\sum_{(g),(\varphi)}\bar{h}(\varphi^{(1)}\rightharpoonup\overline{g^{(1)}})\otimes(\varphi^{(2)}\leftharpoonup\overline{g^{(2)}})\psi=\bar{h}\bar{g}\otimes\varphi\psi,

\displaystyle\Delta(\bar{h}\otimes\varphi)

\displaystyle=\sum(\overline{h^{(1)}}\otimes\rho(\overline{h^{(2)}})^{[-1]}\varphi^{(1)})\otimes(\rho(\overline{h^{(2)}})^{[0]}\otimes\varphi^{(2)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\sum_{(k),(h)}(\overline{h^{(1)}}\otimes\hat{k})\otimes(\overline{S(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)}}\otimes\varphi(k^{(2)}\bullet)),

(96)

and associator

\Phi((\bar{h}\otimes\varphi)\otimes(\bar{g}\otimes\psi)\otimes(\bar{k}\otimes\theta))=\varepsilon_{H}(h)\varepsilon_{H}(g)\varepsilon_{H}(k)\varphi(1_{K})\psi(1_{K})\theta(1_{K}).

Moreover,
(H/K^{+}H)\rtimes\hat{K}
can be equipped with a coquasi-antipode
(S,\alpha,\gamma)
whose existence is guaranteed by the rigidity of the monoidal category
{{}^{H}_{K}}\mathsf{Mod}_{K}
. Thus
(H/K^{+}H)\rtimes\hat{K}
has a structure of coquasi-Hopf algebra. Details can be found in
(
Schauenburg 2002b
, Sec. 4.4)
and
(
Majid 2000
, Sec. 9.4)
.

The equivalence
\tilde{\omega}:{{}^{H/K^{+}H}}\mathsf{Mod}_{K}\simeq{{}^{(H/K^{+}H)\rtimes\hat{K}}\mathsf{Mod}}
is described as follows. Given an
M\in{{}^{H/K^{+}H}}\mathsf{Mod}_{K}
, it can be endowed with a left
(H/K^{+}H)\rtimes\hat{K}
-comodule structure by

N\to((H/K^{+}H)\rtimes\hat{K})\otimes N,\quad n\mapsto\sum_{k,(n)}(n^{[-1]}\otimes\hat{k})\otimes n^{[0]}k,

where
\{k\}
is an orthogonal basis of
K
with
\{\hat{k}\}
its dual basis; conversely, given an
N\in{{}^{{(H/K^{+}H)}\rtimes\hat{K}}}\mathsf{Mod}
with coaction
\rho_{N}(n)=\sum(n^{[-2]}\otimes n^{[-1]})\otimes n^{[0]}\in((H/K^{+}H)\rtimes\hat{K})\otimes N
, it can be endowed with a left
H/K^{+}H
-comodule structure by

N\to(H/K^{+}H)\otimes N,\quad n\mapsto\sum n^{[-2]}\varepsilon(n^{[-1]})\otimes n^{[0]},

and a right
K
-action by
nx=\sum\varepsilon(n^{[-2]})\langle n^{[-1]},x\rangle n^{[0]}
for
x\in K,n\in N
. It is showed that
\tilde{\omega}
is a monoidal equivalence. In summary, there are monoidal equivalences
{{}^{H}_{K}}\mathsf{Mod}_{K}\simeq{{}^{H/K^{+}H}}\mathsf{Mod}_{K}\simeq{{}^{(H/K^{+}H)\rtimes\hat{K}}}\mathsf{Mod}
, see
Schauenburg 2002b
for rigorous proofs. Since
{{}^{A}}\mathsf{Mod}\simeq\mathsf{Mod}_{\hat{A}}\simeq{{}_{\hat{A}^{\rm op}}}\mathsf{Mod}
, we conclude that

{{}^{H}_{K}}\mathsf{Mod}_{K}\simeq{{}_{((H/K^{+}H)\rtimes\hat{K})^{\vee,\rm op}}}\mathsf{Mod}.

(97)

For the convenience of later discussion, consider the identification
{{}^{H}_{K}}\mathsf{Mod}_{K}\simeq{{}^{H^{\rm op}}_{K^{\rm op}}}\mathsf{Mod}_{K^{\rm op}}
and apply the discussion above, we see that
{{}^{H}_{K}}\mathsf{Mod}_{K}\simeq{{}_{\mathcal{K}_{K}}}\mathsf{Mod}
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{K}:

\displaystyle={((H^{\rm op}/K^{\rm op,+}H^{\rm op})\rtimes\widehat{K^{\rm op}})^{\vee,\rm op}}

(98)

\displaystyle\cong((H/HK^{+})^{\rm cop}\rtimes\hat{K}^{\rm cop})^{\rm cop,\vee}

\displaystyle\cong((H/HK^{+})\rtimes\hat{K})^{\vee}.

Therefore, the local operator algebra
\mathcal{D}(s_{b})
is fusion-categorical Morita equivalent to
\mathcal{K}_{K}\cong((H/HK^{+})\rtimes\hat{K})^{\vee}
, which has a structure of quasi-Hopf algebra. This is compatible with Examples
3
and
4
, since
\mathcal{K}_{H}\cong H
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{C}1_{H}}\cong\hat{H}
.
It is important to note that the algebra
\mathcal{K}_{K}
is also fusion-categorical Morita equivalent to the local algebra described in Proposition
6
. This equivalence arises because the category in Eq. (
97
) is equivalent to the fusion category associated with the boundary phase.

3.3.2
Bulk-to-boundary ribbon operators

Let us now consider the construction of the bulk-to-boundary ribbon operator for a gapped boundary determined by a Hopf subalgebra
K\leq H
. The process is similar to that of the bulk case: first, we define the triangle operators on eight types of triangles, then the general ribbon operators are defined recursively. The main tool is the observation that the local operator algebra
\mathcal{D}(s_{b})
is fusion-categorical Morita equivalent to
((H/HK^{+})\otimes\hat{K})^{\vee}\cong(H/HK^{+})^{\vee}\otimes K
given above. Note that
(H/HK^{+})^{\vee}
can be identified with the subspace of
\hat{H}
consisting of those functions vanishing on
HK^{+}
.

Denote
H\mathrm{/\mkern-6.0mu/}K:=H/HK^{+}
.
For
[h]=\bar{h}\in H\mathrm{/\mkern-6.0mu/}K
, define

L^{[h]}_{\pm}|x\rangle:=L^{hh_{K}}_{\pm}|x\rangle,

(99)

where
h_{K}\in K
is the Haar integral of
K
. This is well-defined by the definition of
K^{+}
: if
h=h^{\prime}+h^{\prime\prime}k
with
k\in K^{+}
, then
hh_{K}=h^{\prime}h_{K}+h^{\prime\prime}kh_{K}=h^{\prime}h_{K}+h^{\prime\prime}\varepsilon(k)h_{K}=h^{\prime}h_{K}
. In fact,
H\mathrm{/\mkern-6.0mu/}K\cong Hh_{K},[h]\mapsto hh_{K}
, as left
H
-modules via the restriction of the quotient map
H\to H\mathrm{/\mkern-6.0mu/}K
, whose kernel is
H(1_{H}-h_{K})
. A
\varphi\in\hat{K}
acts on
H
in the following way. First choose a basis
\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}\}
of
H
such that the first
k
vectors form a basis of
K
. Then each dual vector
e^{i}:=\hat{e}_{i}\in\hat{H}
, and so
\varphi=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\varphi(e_{i})e^{i}
can act on
H
by
\varphi\rightharpoonup x
and
x\leftharpoonup\varphi
. Therefore, the edge operators
T_{\pm}^{\varphi}:H\to H
are well-defined for
\varphi\in\hat{K}
.

Ribbon operators are parameterized by pairs
([h],\varphi)
with
[h]\in H\mathrm{/\mkern-6.0mu/}K
and
\varphi\in\hat{K}
. The building blocks are triangle operators. Basically, these operators are similar to Eqs. (
17
–
24
) with some minor modification:
L^{h}_{\pm}
are replaced by
L^{[h]}_{\pm}
. For example,

F^{[h],\varphi}(\tau_{R})|x\rangle=\varepsilon_{H}(h)T_{\pm}^{{\varphi}}|x\rangle,\quad F^{[h],\varphi}(\tilde{\tau}_{R})|x\rangle=\varepsilon_{\hat{K}}(\varphi)L^{[h]}_{\pm}|x\rangle,

(100)

for direct right triangle
\tau_{R}
and dual right triangle
\tilde{\tau}_{R}
, etc.

For a general bulk-to-boundary ribbon
\rho
, say of type-B, ribbon operators are determined by the comultiplication of the dual
\mathcal{D}(s_{b})^{\vee}=(H\mathrm{/\mkern-6.0mu/}K)\otimes\hat{K}
, whose comultiplication is given by (
96
). Since it is a coquasi-Hopf algebra, the comultiplication is co-associative. To define the ribbon operator, take a decomposition
\rho=\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}
. For
[h]\in H\mathrm{/\mkern-6.0mu/}K
and
\varphi\in\hat{K}
, the ribbon operator is then given by

\displaystyle F^{[h],\varphi}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{([h]\otimes\varphi)}F^{([h]\otimes\varphi)^{(1)}}(\rho_{1})F^{([h]\otimes\varphi)^{(2)}}(\rho_{2})

(101)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\sum_{(k),(h)}F^{[h^{(1)}],\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{[S(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)}],\varphi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2}),

where
\{k\}
is a basis of
K
. Note that
[S(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)}]
only depends on
[h]
by simple computation. As in the bulk case, this definition is independent of the choice of the ribbon decomposition. Ribbon operators for ribbons of type-A are defined by
((H\mathrm{/\mkern-6.0mu/}K)\otimes\hat{K})^{\rm op}
in a similar formula.

Remark 3.3
.

If we choose
H=\mathbb{C}[G]
and
K=\mathbb{C}[N]
for a finite group
G
and a subgroup
N\leq G
, then since
\mathbb{C}[G]/\mathbb{C}[G]\mathbb{C}[N]^{+}\cong\mathbb{C}[G/N]
, our definition reduces to the bulk-to-boundary ribbon operator of group-algebra boundary model in Ref.
Cong et al. 2017
. Thus, the group case fits well into our construction.

In Appendix
C
, we have shown that at any boundary site
s_{b}
the following commutation relations hold for
k\in K
and
\psi\in(H\mathrm{/\mkern-6.0mu/}K)^{\vee}
:

\displaystyle A^{k}(s_{b})F^{[h],\varphi}(\rho_{A})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(k)}F^{[h],\varphi(\bullet k^{(2)})}(\rho_{A})A^{k^{(1)}}(s_{b}),

(102)

\displaystyle A^{k}(s_{b})F^{[h],\varphi}(\rho_{B})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(k)}F^{[h],\varphi(\bullet k^{(1)})}(\rho_{B})A^{k^{(2)}}(s_{b}),

(103)

\displaystyle B^{\psi}(s_{b})F^{[h],\varphi}(\rho_{A})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\sum_{\ell,(\ell)}\varphi(\ell^{(2)})F^{[h^{(1)}],\hat{\ell}}(\rho_{A})B^{\psi(S(\ell^{(3)})h^{(2)}\ell^{(1)}\bullet)}(s_{b}),

(104)

\displaystyle B^{\psi}(s_{b})F^{[h],\varphi}(\rho_{B})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\sum_{\ell,(\ell)}\varphi(\ell^{(2)})F^{[h^{(1)}],\hat{\ell}}(\rho_{B})B^{\psi(\bullet S(\ell^{(3)})h^{(2)})\ell^{(1)})}(s_{b}),

(105)

where
\{\ell\}
is a basis of
K
.

The local operator algebra
\mathcal{D}(s_{b})
is fusion-categorical Morita equivalent to
\mathcal{K}_{K}
, which is also fusion-categorical Morita equivalent to
(K\otimes K^{\rm op})\star\hat{H}
by Proposition
6
. The multiplication in the algebra
\mathcal{D}(s_{b})
is determined by the comultiplication of
\mathcal{D}(s_{b})^{\vee}
, hence is given as

(\varphi\otimes x)\cdot(\psi\otimes y)=\sum_{(x)}\varphi\psi(S(x^{(3)})\bullet x^{(1)})\otimes x^{(2)}y,

(106)

by Eq. (
96
).
It is immediate to show that the relation

A^{k}(s_{b})B^{\varphi}(s_{b})=\sum_{(k)}B^{\varphi(S(k^{(3)})\bullet k^{(1)})}(s_{b})A^{k^{(2)}}(s_{b})

(107)

holds for
\varphi\in(H/HK^{+})^{\vee}
and
k\in K
(the proof has no essential difference with that on the bulk). This straightening relation totally determines the multiplication in Eq. (
106
). Hence every boundary site supports a representation of
\mathcal{D}(s_{b})
.

More precisely, consider a bulk-to-boundary ribbon
\rho
with
s_{0}=\partial_{0}\rho
and
s_{1}=\partial_{1}\rho=s_{b}
. Let
|\Omega\rangle
be a ground state. Denote
|\Omega^{[h],\varphi}_{\rho}\rangle=F^{[h],\varphi}(\rho)|\Omega\rangle
and and let
\mathcal{V}(s_{0},s_{1})
denote the space spanned by
\{|\Omega^{[h],\varphi}_{\rho}\rangle\,|\,[h]\in H\mathrm{/\mkern-6.0mu/}K,\,\varphi\in\hat{K}\}
. Then
B^{\psi}(s_{b})A^{k}(s_{b})
defines a representation on
\mathcal{V}(s_{0},s_{1})
. In fact, the commutation relations above imply that

A^{k}(s_{b})|\Omega_{\rho}^{[h],\varphi}\rangle=|\Omega_{\rho}^{[h],\varphi(\bullet k)}\rangle\in\mathcal{V}(s_{0},s_{1}),\;B^{\psi}(s_{b})|\Omega_{\rho}^{[h],\varphi}\rangle=\sum\psi(h^{(2)})|\Omega_{\rho}^{[h^{(1)}],\varphi}\rangle\in\mathcal{V}(s_{0},s_{1});

moreover,

\displaystyle\quad(B^{\psi}(s_{b})A^{k}(s_{b}))((B^{\theta}(s_{b})A^{l}(s_{b}))|\Omega_{\rho}^{[h],\varphi}\rangle)

\displaystyle=\sum\psi(h^{(2)})\theta(h^{(3)})|\Omega_{\rho}^{[h^{(1)}],\varphi(\bullet kl)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\psi(h^{(2)})\theta(S(k^{(3)})h^{(3)}k^{(1)})|\Omega_{\rho}^{[h^{(1)}],\varphi(\bullet k^{(2)}l)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum B^{\psi\theta(S(k^{(3)})\bullet k^{(1)})}(s_{b})A^{k^{(2)}l}(s_{b})|\Omega_{\rho}^{[h],\varphi}\rangle

\displaystyle=((B^{\psi}(s_{b})A^{k}(s_{b}))(B^{\theta}(s_{b})A^{l}(s_{b})))|\Omega_{\rho}^{[h],\varphi}\rangle,

where in the last equality we used Eq. (
107
). In other words, the map

\mathcal{D}(s_{b})\to\operatorname{End}(\mathcal{V}(s_{0},s_{1})),\quad\varphi\otimes k\mapsto B^{\varphi}(s_{b})A^{k}(s_{b})

is an algebra homomorphism and defines the representation.

Example 8
.

Let us take the Kac-Paljutkin algebra
H_{8}
as an example to illustrate our construction.
As an algebra, it is generated by
x,y,z
, with the relations

\displaystyle x^{2}=y^{2}=1,\quad z^{2}=\frac{1}{2}(1+x+y-xy),

\displaystyle xy=yx,\quad zx=yz,\quad zy=xz.

The coalgebra structure and the antipode are determined by

\displaystyle\Delta(x)=x\otimes x,\quad\Delta(y)=y\otimes y,

\displaystyle\Delta(z)=\frac{1}{2}(1\otimes 1+y\otimes 1+1\otimes x-y\otimes x)(z\otimes z),

\displaystyle\varepsilon(x)=\varepsilon(y)=\varepsilon(z)=1,

\displaystyle S(x)=x,\quad S(y)=y,\quad S(z)=z,

with linear extension. Clearly,
H_{8}
has dimension 8 with basis
\{1,x,y,xy,z,zx,zy,zxy\}
.

To classify the gapped boundaries of the
D(H_{8})
quantum double phase, it is necessary to classify the
H_{8}
-comodule algebras (or equivalently
H_{8}
-module algebras) up to Morita equivalence. This classification has been provided in Ref.
(
Etingof et al. 2021
, Sec. 5.3)
.
There are six kinds of gapped boundaries.
Here to illustrate our ribbon operator construction, we only consider one example of
K\leq H_{8}
. By Lagrange theorem,
\dim K
must divide
\dim H_{8}
.
Let us consider the four-dimensional case, which is generated by the group
G=\{1,x,y,xy\}
of group-like elements of
H_{8}
. The corresponding Hopf subalgebra is
K=\mathbb{C}[G]\leq H=H_{8}
.
It is easy to verify that
K
is normal in the sense that
HK^{+}=K^{+}H
, thus
H\mathrm{/\mkern-6.0mu/}K=H/HK^{+}
is a Hopf algebra. Indeed, since
\dim(H/HK^{+})=2
, it is clear that
H/HK^{+}\cong\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
, where one can take
[x]
as a generator. Thus the local operator algebra is fusion-categorical Morita equivalent to
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[G]
.

We can write down the ribbon operators in this case. Note that the Haar integral of
K
is
h_{K}=\frac{1}{4}(1+x+y+xy)
. Let
\delta_{a}
denote the dual element of
a\in K
. The edge operators are

\displaystyle L^{[h]}_{+}|w\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}|hw+hxw+hyw+hxyw\rangle,

\displaystyle L^{[h]}_{-}|w\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}|wh+whx+why+whxy\rangle,

\displaystyle T^{\delta_{a}}_{+}|w\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\delta_{a,w^{(2)}}|w^{(1)}\rangle,

\displaystyle T^{\delta_{a}}_{-}|w\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\delta_{a,w^{(1)}}|w^{(2)}\rangle,

where
\delta_{i,j}=1
if
i=j
and
0
otherwise.
For
[h]\in H/HK^{+}
and
\delta_{a}\in\hat{K}
, the ribbon operator
F^{[h],\delta_{a}}(\rho)
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \hbox to201.84pt{\vbox to59.57pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 52.15964pt\lower-0.8pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

where

\displaystyle a^{\prime}_{1}=a_{1}hh_{K},\quad a^{\prime}_{2}=a_{2}k^{-1}hkh_{K},\quad a^{\prime}_{3}=a_{3}(gk)^{-1}h(gk)h_{K}.

3.3.3
Condensations

Now let us characterize the algebras that give the condensations of the boundary model. One can move bulk excitations to the boundary by applying bulk-to-boundary ribbon operators. Since the stabilizer operators of the Hamiltonian on the boundary are different from those in the bulk, a bulk excitation may be condensed on the boundary sites. Our goal is to determine the subalgebra of the ribbon operator algebra which gives condensations. The group case has been studied in
Beigi et al. 2011
.

Fix a bulk-to-boundary ribbon
\rho=\rho_{\downarrow}
, say of type-B, connecting a bulk site
s
and a boundary site
s_{b}=(v_{b},f_{b})
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{\rho}
be the algebra of ribbon operators
F^{[h],\varphi}(\rho)
, and let
\mathcal{C}_{\rho}\subset\mathcal{A}_{\rho}
be the subalgebra of ribbon operators that commute with both
A^{K}_{v_{b}}
and
B^{H}_{f_{b}}
:

\mathcal{C}_{\rho}=\left\{F\in\mathcal{A}_{\rho}\,|\,[F,A^{K}_{v_{b}}]=[F,B^{H}_{f_{b}}]=0\right\}.

(108)

Applying elements in
\mathcal{C}_{\rho}
to the ground state of the Hamiltonian will create excitations on
s
but not on
s_{b}
.

Let
F=\sum_{h,\varphi}c_{h,\varphi}F^{[h],\varphi}(\rho)
be in
\mathcal{C}_{\rho}
with
c_{h,\varphi}
complex coefficients. By Eq. (
103
),
[F,A^{K}_{v_{b}}]=0
is equivalent to

\sum_{h,\varphi}c_{h,\varphi}F^{[h],\varphi}(\rho)A^{h_{K}}(s_{b})=\sum_{h,\varphi}\sum_{(h_{K})}c_{h,\varphi}F^{[h],\varphi(\bullet h_{K}^{(1)})}(\rho)A^{h_{K}^{(2)}}(s_{b}).

(109)

Multiplying
A^{h_{K}}(s_{b})
on both sides from the right, and using
A^{k}(s_{b})A^{l}(s_{b})=A^{kl}(s_{b})
,
kh_{K}=\varepsilon(k)h_{K}
and
h_{K}^{2}=h_{K}
, the above identity holds if and only if

c_{h,\varphi}=c_{h,\varphi(\bullet h_{K})}.

(110)

Similarly,
[F,B^{H}_{f_{b}}]=0
if and only if
c_{h,\varphi}=0
for
h\notin K
.
By the above computation, we conclude that
\mathcal{C}_{\rho}
is generated by
\{F^{[k],\varphi(\bullet h_{K})}(\rho)~|~k\in K,\varphi\in\hat{K}\}.
This algebra determines all excitations at
s
condensed on the boundary site
s_{b}
.

3.4
Recover the bulk phase from the boundary phase

From the boundary-bulk duality, the bulk phase
\mathcal{D}
can be recovered from boundary phase
\mathcal{B}
by taking monoidal center:
\mathcal{D}\simeq\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{B})
.
It is interesting to consider how to realize this point of view of boundary-bulk duality in the lattice model.
This, to our knowledge, has not been systematically discussed before.

Here we will follow the approach initially proposed by Kong
Kong 2017
;
Kong 2014
to recover the bulk anyon from its boundary condensate in the lattice model.
We know that for bulk anyons
x
, the physical information it carries is: topological charge
x
, fusion rule
[N_{x}]=N_{x,y}^{z}
,
F
-symbol, quantum dimension
d_{x}
, topological spin
\theta_{x}
and braiding
c_{x}=\{c_{x,y}:x\otimes y\to y\otimes x\}_{y}
.
After anyon condensation, the boundary anyon
X=\mathbf{Cond}(x)=x_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus x_{n}
is a condensate of several kinds of bulk anyons and they all lose their braiding information.
To recover the bulk anyons from their boundary condensate, we need to add their braiding information back.
This intuition can be formalized as the following rigorous construction
Turaev and Virelizier 2017
:

Definition 9
.

For a given monoidal category
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathds{1},\alpha,l,r)
, a left half braiding is a pair
(x,\beta_{x,\bullet})
, where
x\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
and
\beta_{x,\bullet}=\{\beta_{x,y}:x\otimes y\to y\otimes x\}_{y\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})}
is a natural isomorphism between functors
x\otimes\bullet
and
\bullet\otimes x
, that is, for any
y,z\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
and
f:y\to z
, we have
(f\otimes\mathrm{id}_{x})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\beta_{x,y}=\beta_{x,z}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}_{x}\otimes f)
, and which is
\otimes
-multiplicative in the sense that for
\forall~y,z\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
, the following diagram commutes:

\begin{split}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 27.14462pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-27.14462pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(x\otimes y)\otimes z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-15.97223pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.52084pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\alpha_{x,y,z}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.19565pt\raise 6.41666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\beta_{x,y}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{z}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 51.14462pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 51.14462pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(y\otimes x)\otimes z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 103.86105pt\raise 5.49306pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.52084pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\alpha_{y,x,z}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 129.43387pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern (111)

The definition of left half braiding implies that
\beta_{x,\mathds{1}}=\mathrm{id}_{x}
; we call
x
the underlying object of the left half braiding
(x,\beta_{x,\bullet})
.

Definition 10
.

The monoidal center
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
of a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
is a category defined as follows.

1.

The objects of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
are left half braidings of
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.,
\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}))=\{(x,\beta_{x,\bullet})\}
;

2.

A morphism
(x,\beta_{x,\bullet})\to(y,\beta_{y,\bullet})
is a morphism
f:x\to y
in
\mathcal{C}
such that for all
z\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
, the following diagram commutes

\begin{split}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern (112)

3.

The unit object of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
is
(\mathds{1},\beta_{\mathds{1},\bullet}=\{\beta_{\mathds{1},x}=\mathrm{id}_{x}\})
;

4.

\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
is a monoidal category: the monoidal structure is given by
(x,\beta_{x,\bullet})\otimes(y,\beta_{y,\bullet})=(x\otimes y,(\beta_{x,\bullet}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{y})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}_{x}\otimes\beta_{y,\bullet}))
, i.e., for any
z\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
, the following diagram commutes

\begin{split}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 129.43387pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 156.57849pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-27.14462pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(x\otimes y)\otimes z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-27.14563pt\raise-15.97223pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 24.20258pt\raise-38.36111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\beta_{x\otimes y,z}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 51.14462pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 103.86105pt\raise-39.05057pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.13391pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\alpha^{-1}_{z,x,y}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 129.43387pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 129.43387pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(z\otimes x)\otimes y}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split};

(113)

5.

\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
is a braided monoidal category: the braiding is given by
c_{(x,\beta_{x,\bullet}),(y,\beta_{y,\bullet})}=\beta_{x,y}
.

By definition, an anyon in the bulk phase (seen from the boundary-to-bulk perspective)
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{B})
is a pair
(X,\beta_{X}=\{\beta_{X,Y}:X\otimes Y\to Y\otimes X\}_{Y\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{B})})
, where
\beta_{X}
is a half braiding.
As illustrated in Fig.
3
, we could consider two situations: (i) the direct condensation of two bulk anyons
x,y
and obtain
X\otimes Y
in the boundary (this is realized by two bulk-to-boundary ribbon operators
F_{X}(\rho^{1}_{\downarrow})
and
F_{Y}(\sigma^{1}_{\downarrow})
with
\rho_{\downarrow}\cap\sigma_{\downarrow}=\emptyset
); and (ii) first braid
x,y
, then condense them and obtain
Y\otimes X
in the boundary (in this case, two corresponding bulk-to-boundary ribbons have non-empty overlap
\rho^{2}_{\downarrow}\cap\sigma^{2}_{\downarrow}\neq\emptyset
).
The braiding information is thus encoded in the morphism

\beta_{X,Y}:X\otimes Y\to Y\otimes X
. Since the boundary phase is described by a UFC
\mathcal{B}
, we could use the boundary ribbon operator
F_{Y}(\xi)
to realize this process.

F_{Y}(\xi)
create
Y,\bar{Y}
pairs at two ends of
\xi
, then we drag
\bar{Y}
to the condensed anyon
Y
and fuse them to vacuum.
Suppose that
|\Omega\rangle
is the ground state, then we have

F_{Y}(\xi)(F_{Y}(\sigma^{1}_{\downarrow})F_{X}(\rho^{1}_{\downarrow})|\Omega\rangle)\cong F_{Y}(\sigma^{2}_{\downarrow})F_{X}(\rho^{2}_{\downarrow})|\Omega\rangle,

(114)

where ‘
\cong
’ means that they are topologically equivalent.

Figure 3:
Illustration of the boundary ribbon operator. The bulk anyon braiding information is contained in the boundary ribbon.

A simple example of toric code is given by Kong
Kong 2017
.
For this case
H=\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
, and the bulk phase is
\mathcal{D}=\{\mathds{1},e,m,\varepsilon\}
.
There are two inequivalent gapped boundaries
Bravyi and Kitaev 1998
: rough boundary
\mathcal{B}_{r}=\{I,M\}
, where
I
is the rough boundary vacuum and
M
is the boundary magnetic charge (bulk anyons
m,\varepsilon
condense to
M
, and
\mathds{1},e
condense to
I
),
and smooth boundary
\mathcal{B}_{s}=\{I,E\}
, where
I
represents the boundary vacuum and
E
represents the boundary electric charge (bulk anyons
e,\varepsilon
condense to
E
, and
\mathds{1},m
condense to
I
).

To recover the bulk phase from the rough boundary, we first need to calculate the monoidal center
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{B}_{r})
.
Each object in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{B}_{r})
is a pair
(x,\beta_{x,\bullet})
. So our goal is to determine each object
x
and isomorphism
\{\beta_{x,y}:x\otimes y\to y\otimes x\}_{y}
corresponding to each bulk anyon.
Notice here
x,y=I,M
; we will give a physical argument based on the lattice model. First, since the bulk particles
\mathds{1}
and
e
condensed as vacuum of
\mathcal{B}_{r}
, they must be of the forms
(I,\beta_{I,\bullet})
and
(I,\rho_{I,\bullet})
; similarly,
m
and
\varepsilon
are of the forms
(M,\beta_{M,\bullet})
and
(M,\rho_{M,\bullet})
. Now we need to specify the half-braidings. Firstly, let us take
m
as an example, the braidings involving
m
are

\begin{split}c_{m,\mathds{1}}&=c_{(M,\beta_{M,\bullet}),(I,\beta_{I,\bullet})}=\beta_{M,I},\\
c_{m,m}&=c_{(M,\beta_{M,\bullet}),(M,\beta_{M,\bullet})}=\beta_{M,M},\\
c_{e,m}&=c_{(I,\rho_{I,\bullet}),(M,\beta_{M,\bullet})}=\rho_{I,M},\\
c_{\varepsilon,m}&=c_{(M,\rho_{M,\bullet}),(M,\beta_{M,\bullet})}=\rho_{M,M}.\end{split}

(115)

If we write down all
c_{x,y}
for
\mathds{1}
,
m
,
e
and
\varepsilon
, comparing with what we have known for
c_{x,y}
, we can easily get the following result
Kong 2017
:

\begin{split}\mathds{1}&=(I,I\otimes I=I\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{I}}I=I\otimes I,I\otimes M=M\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{M}}M=I\otimes M),\\
e&=(I,I\otimes I=I\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{I}}I=I\otimes I,I\otimes M=M\xrightarrow{-\mathrm{id}_{M}}M=I\otimes M),\\
m&=(M,M\otimes I=M\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{M}}M=I\otimes M,M\otimes M=I\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{I}}I=M\otimes M),\\
\varepsilon&=(M,M\otimes I=M\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{M}}M=I\otimes M,M\otimes M=I\xrightarrow{-\mathrm{id}_{I}}I=M\otimes M).\end{split}

(116)

In general, it is believed that braiding has no physical observable effect, we can only observe the mutual statistics that corresponding to double braiding. But we shall argue that for a quantum double with boundary, braiding has its physical reality. This can be seen from toric code boundary model.
We first calculate the statistics of dragging one
e
particle around a boundary
m
vortex. Let
|\Psi(m,f_{b})\rangle
be an excited state containing a magnetic vortex at the boundary face
f_{b}
, that is,
B_{f_{b}}|\Psi\rangle=-|\Psi\rangle
.
The boundary ribbon operator
F_{e}(\xi)
in this case is just a
Z
-string:

F_{e}(\xi)=\prod_{j\in\xi}\sigma^{z}_{j}.

(117)

Notice that
f_{b}
must be surrounded by
\xi
.
When implementing this on the boundary excited state
|\Psi(m,f_{b})\rangle
, we have

|\Psi(m,f_{b})\rangle\mapsto F_{e}(\xi)|\Psi(m,f_{b})\rangle=\prod_{f\,\mathrm{inside}\,\xi}B_{f}|\Psi(m,f_{b})\rangle=-|\Psi(m,f_{b})\rangle.

(118)

The boundary ribbon operator
F_{e}(\xi)
realizes the half braiding between
m
and
e
and it has a physical observable effect.
All other half braidings can be realized in a similar way.

For the general Hopf quantum double boundary, the generalization is straightforward.
To recover the bulk phase from the boundary phase, we implement a boundary-to-bulk ribbon operator
F_{X}(\rho_{\uparrow})
which connects boundary site
s_{b}

and bulk site
s
. At boundary site
s_{b}
, there is a boundary excitation
X
which is a condensate of several bulk anyons
x_{i}
(e.g.,
M
is a condensate of
m,\varepsilon
in toric code).
We have a collection of boundary ribbon operators

\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{B}}(\xi)=\{F_{Y}(\xi),Y\in\mathcal{B}\}.

(119)

Choosing different values from equivalent classes of
[h],[\varphi]
for
F_{X}(\rho_{\uparrow})
we obtain different bulk anyons.

4
Gapped domain wall theory

Let us now consider the gapped domain wall, which is closely related to the gapped boundary: the gapped boundary can be regarded as a domain wall that separates the Hopf quantum double phase from the vacuum phase; conversely, using the folding trick, a gapped domain wall can be transformed into a gapped boundary.
In this section, let us generalize the construction of the lattice model for gapped boundaries to the case of general domain walls and examine the correspondence between boundaries and domain walls.
We will see that the domain wall is characterized by an
H_{1}|H_{2}
-bicomodule algebra
\mathfrak{B}
(or equivalently
H_{1}|H_{2}
-bimodule algebra
\mathfrak{N}
):

Definition 11
.

Let
H_{1},H_{2}
be Hopf algebras,
\mathfrak{B}
be an algebra, and
\mathfrak{B}
be an
H_{1}|H_{2}
-bicomodule with coaction
\beta_{\mathfrak{B}}:\mathfrak{B}\to H_{1}\otimes\mathfrak{B}\otimes H_{2}
. If
\beta_{\mathfrak{B}}
is a homomorphism of algebras, then
\mathfrak{B}
is called an
H_{1}|H_{2}
-bicomodule algebra.

Remark 4.1
.

For an
H_{1}|H_{2}
-bicomodule algebra
\mathfrak{B}
with coaction
\beta_{\mathfrak{B}}
, the Sweedler’s notation reads
\beta_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)=\sum_{[x]}x^{[-1]}\otimes x^{[0]}\otimes x^{[1]}\in H_{1}\otimes\mathfrak{B}\otimes H_{2}
.

Definition 12
.

Let
H_{1},H_{2}
be Hopf algebras,
\mathfrak{N}
be an algebra, and
\mathfrak{N}
be an
H_{1}|H_{2}
-bimodule.
\mathfrak{N}
is called a bimodule algebra if
h\triangleright(xy)\triangleleft g=\sum_{(h),(g)}(h^{(1)}\triangleright x\triangleleft g^{(2)})(h^{(2)}\triangleright y\triangleleft g^{(1)})
.

4.1
Domain wall lattice

(a)

(b)

Figure 4:
The depictions of (a) the domain wall, domain wall face, domain wall vertex, and domain wall site, and (b) the folding trick.

Suppose that two
2d
quantum double phases, which are respectively characterized by Hopf algebras
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
, are separated by a
1d
domain wall, see Fig.
4
(a). Suppose that the domain wall is determined by an
H_{1}|H_{2}
-bicomodule algebra
\mathfrak{B}
. The boundary lattice realization in Sec.
3.1.2
can be generalized to domain wall straightforwardly. By convention, when walking along the direction on the domain wall edges, the
H_{1}
-bulk is always on the left-hand side while the
H_{2}
-bulk is always on the right-hand side. According to how the edges on the two bulks are connected to the domain wall vertices, there are four configurations. There are four corresponding sites for each of these configurations. We define the domain wall vertex operator
A^{a\otimes b}(s_{d_{i}})
(
i=1,2
) for
