\displaystyle\quad\quad A^{a\otimes b}(s_{d_{i}})|x,k,y,h\rangle=\sum_{[a]}|a^{[0]}x,a^{[-1]}k,yb,a^{[1]}h\rangle.

(127)

The convention follows that in Sec.
2.1
.

To construct the lattice model of the domain wall, choose the symmetric separability idempotent
\lambda\in\mathfrak{B}\otimes\mathfrak{B}^{\rm op}
of the algebra
\mathfrak{B}
to form the domain wall vertex operator
A^{\lambda}(s_{d_{i}})
.
The domain wall Hamiltonian is thus given as

H[C(\Sigma_{d})]=\sum_{i=1,2}\sum_{d_{i}}(I-A^{\lambda}(s_{d_{i}}))+\sum_{i=1,2}\sum_{d_{i}}(I-B^{\varphi_{\hat{H}_{i}}}(s_{d_{i}})).

(128)

4.2
Topological excitation of the domain wall

In this part, we will establish the algebraic theory of the gapped domain wall and discuss the relation between the gapped domain wall and the gapped boundary in the language of Hopf algebras. The main results are summarized in Table
4
.
Similar to the boundary, we can analyze the domain wall excitations at two different but equivalent levels:

•

From the anyon condensation perspective, a domain wall that separates two bulks with topological order given by UMTCs
\mathcal{D}_{i}
(
i=1,2
) is a UFC
\mathcal{W}
.
The anyon condensation from
\mathcal{D}_{i}
to domain wall
\mathcal{W}
is controlled by a condensable algebra
A_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{i}
(i.e., a connected separable commutative algebra)
Kong 2014
. The wall excitations are given by
\mathcal{W}=\mathsf{Mod}_{A_{i}}(\mathcal{D}_{i})
.

•

From the bulk UFC
\mathcal{C}_{i}
(
i=1,2
) perspective, two bulk topological order are given by
\mathcal{D}_{i}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{i})
.
The domain wall is characterized by a
\mathcal{C}_{1}|\mathcal{C}_{2}
-bimodule category
\mathcal{M}
.
The domain wall excitations are given by the category of all module functors
\mathcal{W}=\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}|\mathcal{C}_{2}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M})
.
Notice that in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
bulk,
\mathcal{C}_{i}
can be regarded as a
\mathcal{C}_{i}|\mathcal{C}_{i}
-bimodule category; thus the bulk topological excitations can also be described by
\mathcal{D}_{i}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{i})\simeq\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}|\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i})
.

For our construction, two bulk UFCs are
\mathcal{C}_{i}=\mathsf{Rep}(H_{i})
, and
the domain wall is characterized by an
H_{1}|H_{2}
-bicomodule algebra
\mathfrak{B}
.
The category
{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod}
is a
\mathsf{Rep}(H_{1})|\mathsf{Rep}(H_{2})
-bimodule category. To see this, we only need to check that
for
X_{i}\in\mathsf{Rep}(H_{i})
and
M\in{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod}
,
X_{1}\otimes M\otimes X_{2}
is also a
\mathfrak{B}
-module.
In fact, it is easy to verify that the module structure map is given by (in diagrammatic representation)

\mu_{X_{1}\otimes M\otimes X_{2}}=\begin{aligned} \hbox to112.4pt{\vbox to117.43pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 17.78868pt\lower-78.63156pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{}{}{}{}}}}{}

(129)

viz.
,
\mu_{X_{1}\otimes M\otimes X_{2}}=(\mu_{X_{1}}\otimes\mu_{M}\otimes\mu_{X_{2}})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}_{H_{1}}\otimes\tau_{\mathfrak{B},X_{1}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M\otimes H_{2}\otimes X_{2}})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}_{H_{1}\otimes\mathfrak{B}\otimes X_{1}}\otimes\tau_{H_{2},M}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X_{2}})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}_{H_{1}\otimes\mathfrak{B}}\otimes\tau_{H_{2},X_{1}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M\otimes X_{2}})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\beta_{\mathfrak{B}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X_{1}\otimes M\otimes X_{2}})
.
This proves that the boundary bimodule category for our model is
\mathcal{M}\simeq{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod}
.

We can also apply the folding trick to our model. As illustrated in Fig.
4
(b), an
H_{1}|H_{2}
gapped domain wall can be transformed into an
H_{1}\otimes H^{\rm cop}_{2}
gapped boundary.
The
H_{1}|H_{2}
-bicomodule algebra
\mathfrak{B}
now becomes an
H_{1}\otimes H_{2}^{\rm cop}
-left comodule algebra.
All the results about boundary topological excitations can be applied to the domain wall. Conversely, the gapped boundary can be regarded as a gapped domain wall between Hopf algebras
H_{1}=H
and vacuum
H_{2}=\mathbb{C}
.
From this, we see that the gapped boundary theory and gapped domain wall theory for Hopf quantum double phase are dual to each other.

Proposition 7
.

The topological excitations of the gapped domain wall characterized by an
H_{1}|H_{2}
-bicomodule algebra
\mathfrak{B}
are equivalently described by the following categories:

(i)

the category of
\mathsf{Rep}(H_{1})|\mathsf{Rep}(H_{2})
-bimodule functors
\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathsf{Rep}(H_{1})|\mathsf{Rep}(H_{2})}({{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod},{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod})
;

(ii)

the category of
\mathsf{Rep}(H_{1}\otimes H_{2}^{\rm cop})
-module functors
\mathcal{W}\simeq\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathsf{Rep}(H_{1}\otimes H_{2}^{\rm cop})}({{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod},{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod})
;

(iii)

the category of all
\mathfrak{B}|\mathfrak{B}
-bimodules
{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{B}}
.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward by using the result for boundary topological excitations and the folding trick.
∎

The dictionary between Hopf quantum double model and string-net model for gapped domain wall.

Now consider an
H_{1}|H_{2}
-bicomodule algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,
{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod}
is a
\mathsf{Rep}(H_{1})|\mathsf{Rep}(H_{2})
-bimodule category.
If we reverse the direction of the domain wall, we obtain an
H_{2}|H_{1}
-domain wall. This domain wall is given by the opposite category
{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod}^{\rm op}
, which is a
\mathsf{Rep}(H_{2})|\mathsf{Rep}(H_{1})
-bimodule category.
If we have

{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod}^{\rm op}\boxtimes_{\mathsf{Rep}(H_{1})}{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod}\simeq\mathsf{Rep}(H_{2}),\quad{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod}\boxtimes_{\mathsf{Rep}(H_{2})}{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod}^{\rm op}\simeq\mathsf{Rep}(H_{1}),

(130)

this domain wall will be called a transparent domain wall (the corresponding bimodule category is called invertible).
The box tensor here is the Deligne tensor product, which means that we fuse two domain walls over some given bulk that is in between them.

5
Ground states in the presence of boundaries and domain walls

In this section, we will solve the extended generalized quantum double model with gapped boundaries and domain walls and obtain the corresponding ground states.
To this end, we first review the Hopf tensor network representation of the states proposed in
Buerschaper et al. 2013a
, which is convenient for investigating the entanglement properties of the states.

5.1
Hopf tensor network states

Consider two Hopf algebras
J,W
with a pairing
\langle\bullet,\bullet\rangle:J\otimes W\to\mathbb{C}
and
\phi\in J,x\in W
.
The basic ingredient of the Hopf tensor network is the rank-2 Hopf tensor
\Psi(x,\phi)=\langle\phi,x\rangle
,

(132)

Notice that here the blue edges (corresponding to the faces of the lattice) are labeled with
\phi\in J
and the black edges (corresponding to the edges of the lattice) are labeled with
x\in W
. For fixed
\phi,x
, the evaluation is determined by the pairing, and the process will be called Hopf trace.
Therefore, the Hopf tensor network representation is a diagrammatic representation of the pairing.

For two Hopf algebras
J
and
I
which both have their pairings with Hopf algebra
W
, we can contract the rank-2 tensors to obtain rank-3 tensors.
There are two types of contractions. The first one is parallel gluing, which is determined by the comultiplication of
W
.
For example, fix
\phi\in J
,
\psi\in I
and
x\in W
, we define

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} {{}{}{{

{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{54.27472pt}{55.72496pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{aligned}=\sum_{(\phi)}\langle\phi^{(1)},x\rangle\langle\phi^{(2)},y\rangle.

(134)

Notice that when dealing with a face, the starting site determined the starting point of ordered pairing according to Eq. (
134
).

With the above rules, for arbitrary Hopf tensor network
\Gamma
with no free indices (which is a lattice for which all edges and faces have been labeled, and each face has its fixed starting site), the above Hopf trace gives us a complex number. We denote the Hopf trace as

\Psi_{\Gamma}(\{x_{e}\}_{e\in E_{\Gamma}},\{\phi_{f}\}_{f\in F_{\Gamma}})=\mathrm{ttr}_{\Gamma}(\{x_{e}\}_{e\in E_{\Gamma}},\{\phi_{f}\}_{f\in F_{\Gamma}}),

(135)

whose evaluation rules are given by Eqs. (
133
) and (
134
).

Now consider a lattice
C(\Sigma)
of a
2d
surface
\Sigma
with face set
F
and edge set
E
. We assign each face
f
a Hopf algebra
J_{f}
and each edge
e
a Hopf algebra
W_{e}
. Assume that for each face,
J_{f}
has the corresponding pairings with the edge Hopf algebras for edges
e\in\partial f
.
If we set the edge with values
x_{e}\in W_{e}
and face with values
\phi_{f}\in J_{f}
, then we obtain a Hopf tensor network

(\otimes_{e\in E}x_{e})\otimes(\otimes_{f\in F}\phi_{f})\mapsto\Psi_{C(\Sigma)}(\{x_{e}\},\{\phi_{f}\})=\mathrm{ttr}_{C(\Sigma)}(\{x_{e}\},\{\phi_{f}\}).

(136)

The corresponding Hopf tensor network states are defined as

|\Psi_{C(\Sigma)}(\{x_{e}\},\{\phi_{f}\})\rangle=\sum_{(x_{e})}\mathrm{ttr}_{C(\Sigma)}(\{x_{e}^{(2)}\},\{\phi_{f}\})\bigotimes_{e\in E}|x_{e}^{(1)}\rangle.

(137)

5.2
Ground state of the boundary model

From an anyon-condensation point of view, the bulk phase of the Hopf algebraic quantum double model is characterized by the UMTC
\mathcal{B}=\mathsf{Rep}(D(H))\simeq\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Rep}(H))
.
A gapped boundary is characterized by a Lagrangian algebra
L
in
\mathcal{B}
.
For a sphere
\Sigma
with
n
boundaries
\partial^{i}\Sigma
and each boundary is characterized by its Lagrangian algebra
L_{i}
, the ground state degeneracy is given by

\operatorname{GSD}=\dim\mathrm{Hom}(\mathds{1},L_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes L_{n}),

(138)

where
\mathds{1}
is the bulk vacuum charge.
For the disk, there is only one boundary. From the fact that, for arbitrary Lagrangian algebra,
\dim\mathrm{Hom}(\mathds{1},L)=1
, this implies that the ground state on a disk is unique. Notice that Eq. (
138
) is universal, namely, it is independent of the lattice realizations.

Let us solve the Hamiltonian of the boundary lattice model constructed in Sec.
3.1.2
in terms of the Hopf tensor network states. For the boundary model determined by a right
H
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
, the bulk edge algebra is
H_{e}=H
, the bulk face algebra is
\hat{H}_{f}=\hat{H}
and the boundary edge algebra is
\mathfrak{A}_{e_{b}}=\mathfrak{A}
. Recall that each
\varphi\in\hat{H}
can act on
\mathfrak{A}
by the coaction:
\varphi\rightharpoonup x=\sum x^{[0]}\varphi(x^{[1]})
, since
x^{[1]}\in H
. Hence for
h_{e}\in H_{e}
,
\phi_{f},\,\varphi_{f_{b}}\in\hat{H}_{f}
, and
x_{e_{b}}\in\mathfrak{A}_{e_{b}}
, we have the following Hopf tensor network state

\displaystyle|\Psi_{C(\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma);C(\partial\Sigma)}(\{h_{e}\},\{\phi_{f}\};\{x_{e_{b}}\},\{\varphi_{f_{b}}\})\rangle

(139)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h_{e})}\sum_{[x_{e_{b}}]}\operatorname{ttr}_{C(\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma);C(\partial\Sigma)}(\{h_{e}^{(2)}\},\{\phi_{f}\};\{x_{e_{b}}^{[1]}\},\{\varphi_{f_{b}}\})\bigotimes_{e\in E(\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma)}|h_{e}^{(1)}\rangle\bigotimes_{e_{b}\in E(\partial\Sigma)}|x_{e_{b}}^{[0]}\rangle.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{A}
is augmented, i.e., there is an algebra homomorphism
\varepsilon:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathbb{C}
; for example, one can take the counit
\varepsilon
when
\mathfrak{A}=K
is a Hopf subalgebra. Let
\lambda=\sum_{\langle\lambda\rangle}\lambda^{\langle 1\rangle}\otimes\lambda^{\langle 2\rangle}\in\mathfrak{A}\otimes\mathfrak{A}^{\rm op}
be the symmetric separability idempotent of
\mathfrak{A}
. Denote

h_{\mathfrak{A}}:=\sum_{\langle\lambda\rangle}\lambda^{\langle 1\rangle}\varepsilon(\lambda^{\langle 2\rangle})\in\mathfrak{A}.

(140)

When
\mathfrak{A}=K
and
\varepsilon
is the counit of
K
,
h_{\mathfrak{A}}
is the Haar integral of
K
.

Proposition 8

(Ground state on a disk)
.

Suppose that
\Sigma
is a disk, then the ground state is unique. Then the ground state of the boundary lattice model determined by an
H
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
is the Hopf tensor network state

|\Psi_{GS}\rangle=|\Psi_{C(\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma);C(\partial\Sigma)}(\{h_{H,e}\},\{\phi_{\hat{H},f}\};\{h_{\mathfrak{A},e_{b}}\},\{\varphi_{\hat{H},f_{b}}\})\rangle.

(141)

For each bulk site
s
, we have

B^{\varphi}(s)A^{g}(s)|\Psi_{GS}\rangle=\varepsilon(g)\varphi(1_{H})|\Psi_{GS}\rangle.

(142)

Proof.

The first assertion follows from the general equation (
138
). Hence by the uniqueness of the ground state on a disk, it suffices to check the state (
141
) satisfies the stabilizer conditions. The proof for the bulk part is the same as that in Ref.
Buerschaper et al. 2013a
. Let us prove the boundary part.

Consider the following configuration

\displaystyle\hbox to75.01pt{\vbox to115.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 17.90297pt\lower-0.8pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 39.37 39.37 M 74.8 39.37 C 74.8 58.94 58.94 74.8 39.37 74.8 C 19.8 74.8 3.94 58.94 3.94 39.37 C 3.94 19.8 19.8 3.94 39.37 3.94 C 58.94 3.94 74.8 19.8 74.8 39.37 Z M 39.37 39.37}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 15.2 13.78 L 17.11 11.65}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-0.66898}{0.74329}{-0.74329}{-0.66898}{12.36691pt}{8.42076pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.98 0 C 3.51 0.28 1.11 1.11 -0.55 2.08 L -0.55 -2.08 C 1.11 -1.11 3.51 -0.28 4.98 0}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 39.37 118.11 M 74.8 118.11 C 74.8 137.68 58.94 153.54 39.37 153.54 C 19.8 153.54 3.94 137.68 3.94 118.11 C 3.94 98.54 19.8 82.68 39.37 82.68 C 58.94 82.68 74.8 98.54 74.8 118.11 Z M 39.37 118.11}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

In this case, the state (
141
) near the vertex reads

\displaystyle|\Psi_{v_{b}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi_{1}(S(h_{H}^{(3)})h_{1}^{[1]})\varphi_{2}(h_{2}^{[1]}h_{H}^{(2)})|h_{1}^{[0]},h_{H}^{(1)},h_{2}^{[0]}\rangle

(143)

\displaystyle=\sum\varepsilon(\lambda_{1}^{\langle 2\rangle})\varepsilon(\lambda_{2}^{\langle 2\rangle})\varphi_{1}(S(h_{H}^{(3)})\lambda_{1}^{\langle 1\rangle[1]})\varphi_{2}(\lambda_{2}^{\langle 1\rangle[1]}h_{H}^{(2)})|\lambda_{1}^{\langle 1\rangle[0]},h_{H}^{(1)},\lambda_{2}^{\langle 1\rangle[0]}\rangle,

where
\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{2}=\varphi_{\hat{H}}
,
h_{1}=h_{2}=h_{\mathfrak{A}}
, and
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=\lambda
. One computes

\displaystyle A^{\lambda}(s_{b})|\Psi_{v_{b}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varepsilon(\lambda_{1}^{\langle 2\rangle})\varepsilon(\lambda_{2}^{\langle 2\rangle})\varphi_{1}(S(h_{H}^{(3)})\lambda_{1}^{\langle 1\rangle[1]})\varphi_{2}(\lambda_{2}^{\langle 1\rangle[1]}h_{H}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad|\lambda^{\langle 1\rangle}\lambda_{1}^{\langle 1\rangle[0]},S(\lambda^{\langle 2\rangle[1]})h_{H}^{(1)},\lambda_{2}^{\langle 1\rangle[0]}\lambda^{\langle 2\rangle[0]}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varepsilon(\lambda_{1}^{\langle 2\rangle})\varepsilon(\lambda_{2}^{\langle 2\rangle})\varphi_{1}(S(h_{H}^{(3)})S(\lambda^{\langle 1\rangle[2]})\lambda^{\langle 1\rangle[1]}\lambda_{1}^{\langle 1\rangle[1]})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\varphi_{2}(\lambda_{2}^{\langle 1\rangle[1]}h_{H}^{(2)})|\lambda^{\langle 1\rangle[0]}\lambda_{1}^{\langle 1\rangle[0]},S(\lambda^{\langle 2\rangle[1]})h_{H}^{(1)},\lambda_{2}^{\langle 1\rangle[0]}\lambda^{\langle 2\rangle[0]}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varepsilon(\lambda_{1}^{\langle 2\rangle}\lambda^{\langle 1\rangle[0]})\varepsilon(\lambda_{2}^{\langle 2\rangle})\varphi_{1}(S(h_{H}^{(3)})S(\lambda^{\langle 1\rangle[1]})\lambda_{1}^{\langle 1\rangle[1]})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\varphi_{2}(\lambda_{2}^{\langle 1\rangle[1]}h_{H}^{(2)})|\lambda_{1}^{\langle 1\rangle[0]},S(\lambda^{\langle 2\rangle[1]})h_{H}^{(1)},\lambda_{2}^{\langle 1\rangle[0]}\lambda^{\langle 2\rangle[0]}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varepsilon(\lambda_{1}^{\langle 2\rangle}\lambda^{\langle 1\rangle[0]})\varepsilon(\lambda_{2}^{\langle 2\rangle})\varphi_{1}(S(\lambda^{\langle 2\rangle[2]}h_{H}^{(3)})S(\lambda^{\langle 1\rangle[1]})\lambda_{1}^{\langle 1\rangle[1]})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\varphi_{2}(\lambda_{2}^{\langle 1\rangle[1]}\lambda^{\langle 2\rangle[1]}h_{H}^{(2)})|\lambda_{1}^{\langle 1\rangle[0]},h_{H}^{(1)},\lambda_{2}^{\langle 1\rangle[0]}\lambda^{\langle 2\rangle[0]}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varepsilon(\lambda_{1}^{\langle 2\rangle}\lambda^{\langle 1\rangle[0]})\varepsilon(\lambda^{\langle 2\rangle[0]}\lambda_{2}^{\langle 2\rangle})\varphi_{1}(S(h_{H}^{(3)})S(\lambda^{\langle 1\rangle[1]}\lambda^{\langle 2\rangle[1]})\lambda_{1}^{\langle 1\rangle[1]})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\varphi_{2}(\lambda_{2}^{\langle 1\rangle[1]}h_{H}^{(2)})|\lambda_{1}^{\langle 1\rangle[0]},h_{H}^{(1)},\lambda_{2}^{\langle 1\rangle[0]}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varepsilon(\lambda_{1}^{\langle 2\rangle}\lambda^{\langle 1\rangle})\varepsilon(\lambda^{\langle 2\rangle}\lambda_{2}^{\langle 2\rangle})\varphi_{1}(S(h_{H}^{(3)})\lambda_{1}^{\langle 1\rangle[1]})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\varphi_{2}(\lambda_{2}^{\langle 1\rangle[1]}h_{H}^{(2)})|\lambda_{1}^{\langle 1\rangle[0]},h_{H}^{(1)},\lambda_{2}^{\langle 1\rangle[0]}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varepsilon(\lambda_{1}^{\langle 2\rangle})\varepsilon(\lambda_{2}^{\langle 2\rangle})\varphi_{1}(S(h_{H}^{(3)})\lambda_{1}^{\langle 1\rangle[1]})\varphi_{2}(\lambda_{2}^{\langle 1\rangle[1]}h_{H}^{(2)})|\lambda_{1}^{\langle 1\rangle[0]},h_{H}^{(1)},\lambda_{2}^{\langle 1\rangle[0]}\rangle,

where the third equality follows from
\sum x\lambda_{1}^{\langle 1\rangle}\otimes\lambda_{1}^{\langle 2\rangle}=\sum\lambda_{1}^{\langle 1\rangle}\otimes\lambda^{\langle 2\rangle}x
, the fourth from
\sum S(h)h_{H}^{(1)}\otimes h_{H}^{(2)}=\sum h_{H}^{(1)}\otimes hh_{H}^{(2)}
, the fifth from
\sum\lambda_{2}^{\langle 1\rangle}x\otimes\lambda_{1}^{\langle 2\rangle}=\sum\lambda_{2}^{\langle 1\rangle}\otimes x\lambda_{2}^{\langle 2\rangle}
, the sixth from Eq. (
41
), and the last from
\sum\lambda^{\langle 1\rangle}\lambda^{\langle 2\rangle}=1
.

In this case, the state (
141
) near the face is

\displaystyle|\Psi_{f_{b}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi_{1}(h_{1}^{(2)})\varphi_{2}(S(h_{2}^{(3)}))\varphi_{3}(S(h_{3}^{(3)}))\varphi(h^{[1]}S(h_{1}^{(3)})h_{2}^{(2)}h_{3}^{(2)})

(144)

\displaystyle|h^{[0]},h_{1}^{(1)},h_{2}^{(1)},h_{3}^{(1)}\rangle,

where
h_{i}=h_{H}
,
\varphi_{i}=\varphi=\varphi_{\hat{H}}
, and
h=h_{\mathfrak{A}}
. By computation, one has for
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi_{\hat{H}}
,

\displaystyle B_{f_{b}}^{H}|\Psi_{f_{b}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi_{1}(h_{1}^{(3)})\varphi_{2}(S(h_{2}^{(4)}))\varphi_{3}(S(h_{3}^{(4)}))\varphi(h^{[2]}S(h_{1}^{(4)})h_{2}^{(3)}h_{3}^{(3)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\varphi^{\prime}(h^{[1]}S(h_{1}^{(1)})h_{2}^{(2)}h_{3}^{(2)})|h^{[0]},h_{1}^{(2)},h_{2}^{(1)},h_{3}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi_{1}(h_{1}^{(1)})\varphi_{2}(S(h_{2}^{(4)}))\varphi_{3}(S(h_{3}^{(4)}))\varphi(h^{[2]}S(h_{1}^{(2)})h_{2}^{(3)}h_{3}^{(3)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\varphi^{\prime}(h^{[1]}S(h_{1}^{(3)})h_{2}^{(2)}h_{3}^{(2)})|h^{[0]},h_{1}^{(4)},h_{2}^{(1)},h_{3}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi_{1}(h_{1}^{(1)})\varphi_{2}(S(h_{2}^{(3)}))\varphi_{3}(S(h_{3}^{(3)}))\varphi(h^{[1]}S(h_{1}^{(2)})h_{2}^{(2)}h_{3}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad|h^{[0]},h_{1}^{(3)},h_{2}^{(1)},h_{3}^{(1)}\rangle,

where the last equality follows from
\varphi_{\hat{H}}^{2}=\varphi_{\hat{H}}
. The last assertion is immediate from the property of Haar integral.
∎

In Appendix
D
, we give an alternative construction of the boundary lattice model based on the generalized quantum double. We can also solve the Hamiltonian of this kind of boundary model by the Hopf tensor network states. For this construction, all internal edges are set as the Hopf algebra
H
, and internal faces the Hopf algebra
\hat{H}
. The boundary edge is set as
W
and the boundary face is set as
J
.
In this way, we obtain the corresponding Hopf tensor network states of the model,

\displaystyle|\Psi_{C(\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma);C(\partial\Sigma)}(\{x_{e}\},\{\phi_{f}\};\{y_{e_{b}}\},\varphi_{f_{b}})\rangle

(145)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(x_{e}),(y_{e_{b}})}\mathrm{ttr}_{C(\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma);C(\partial\Sigma)}(\{x_{e}^{(2)}\},\{\phi_{f}\};\{y_{e_{b}}^{(2)}\},\varphi_{f_{b}})\bigotimes_{e\in E(\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma)}|x_{e}^{(1)}\rangle\bigotimes_{e_{b}\in E(\partial\Sigma)}|y_{e_{b}}^{(1)}\rangle.

The ground state of the model is the one for which we choose all elements as Haar integrals.

Proposition 9

(Ground state on a disk)
.

Suppose that
\Sigma
is a disk, then the ground state is unique.
The ground state of the extended generalized quantum double model is the Hopf tensor network state with all edge and face elements chosen as Haar integrals

|\Psi_{GS}\rangle=|\Psi_{C(\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma);C(\partial\Sigma)}(\{h_{H,e}\},\{\varphi_{\hat{H},f}\};\{h_{K,e_{b}}\},\{\varphi_{J,f_{b}}\})\rangle.

(146)

For each bulk site
s
, we have

B^{\varphi}(s)A^{g}(s)|\Psi_{GS}\rangle=\varepsilon(g)\varphi(1_{H})|\Psi_{GS}\rangle.

(147)

For the boundary site
s_{b}
, we have

B^{\varphi}(s_{b})A^{g}(s_{b})|\Psi_{GS}\rangle=\varepsilon(g)\varphi(1_{H})|\Psi_{GS}\rangle.

(148)

Proof.

Since the ground state is unique, we only need to check that the state we construct here satisfies the stabilizer conditions.
For the bulk part, the proof is the same as given in Ref.
Buerschaper et al. 2013a
.
We only need to check the boundary stabilizers.
Recall that in our construction, the boundary can be treated as a big face (see Appendix
D
). Using the fact that
\varphi_{J}\varphi_{J}=\varphi_{J}
and
h_{W}\in\operatorname{Cocom}(W)
, it is easy to check
B^{\varphi_{J}}|\Psi_{GS}\rangle=|\Psi_{GS}\rangle
.
For vertex stabilizers, using the fact that
h_{K}\in\operatorname{Comm}(K)
,
h_{W}\in\operatorname{Cocom}(W)
and
kh_{W}=\varepsilon(k)h_{W}=h_{W}k
, we have
A^{h_{K}}|\Psi_{GS}\rangle=|\Psi_{GS}\rangle
.
Eqs. (
147
) and (
148
) are direct consequences of the definition of Haar integral.
∎

5.3
Ground state of the domain wall model

Using the Hopf tensor network representation, we can also solve the model with a domain wall
\Sigma_{d}
separating two bulks
\Sigma_{i}
(
i=1,2
).
The spirit is the same as the construction of the boundary. Consider the domain wall determined by an
H_{1}|H_{2}
-bicomodule algebra
\mathfrak{B}
, constructed in Sec.
4
. For
h_{i,e_{i}}\in H_{e_{i}}
,
\phi_{i,f_{i}},\,\varphi_{i,d_{i}}\in\hat{H}_{f_{i}}
(
i=1,2
), and
x_{e_{d}}\in\mathfrak{B}
, we have the corresponding Hopf tensor network state

\displaystyle|\Psi_{C(\Sigma_{1}),C(\Sigma_{d}),C(\Sigma_{2})}(\{h_{1,e_{1}}\},\{\phi_{1,f_{1}}\};\{\varphi_{1,d_{1}}\},\{x_{e_{d}}\},\{\varphi_{2,d_{2}}\};\{h_{2,e_{2}}\},\{\phi_{2,f_{2}}\})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\operatorname{ttr}_{C(\Sigma_{1}),C(\Sigma_{d}),C(\Sigma_{2})}(\{h_{1,e_{1}}^{(2)}\},\{\phi_{1,f_{1}}\};\{\varphi_{1,d_{1}}\},\{x_{e_{d}}^{[-1]}\};\{\varphi_{2,d_{2}}\},\{x_{e_{d}}^{[1]}\};\{h_{2,e_{2}}^{(2)}\},\{\phi_{2,f_{2}}\})

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{e_{1}\in E(\Sigma_{1})}|h_{1,e_{1}}^{(1)}\rangle\bigotimes_{e_{d}\in E(\Sigma_{d})}|x^{[0]}_{e_{d}}\rangle\bigotimes_{e_{2}\in E(\Sigma_{2})}|h^{(1)}_{2,e_{2}}\rangle,

where
e_{i}
and
f_{i}
are the edges and faces respectively in region
i=1,2
,
e_{d}
are edges on the wall, and
f_{d_{i}}
are wall faces in region
i=1,2
. By a similar computation as in Proposition
8
, one can show that the ground state
|\Psi_{GS}\rangle
is given by

|\Psi_{C(\Sigma_{1}),C(\Sigma_{d}),C(\Sigma_{2})}(\{h_{H_{1},e_{1}}\},\{\phi_{\hat{H}_{1},f_{1}}\};\{\varphi_{\hat{H}_{1},d_{1}}\},\{h_{\mathfrak{B},e_{d}}\},\{\varphi_{\hat{H}_{2},d_{2}}\};\{h_{H_{2},e_{2}}\},\{\phi_{\hat{H}_{2},f_{2}}\})\rangle.

In Appendix
E
, we construct a domain wall lattice model based on pairings between Hopf algebras. We can apply the same method to identify the ground state of this type of domain wall model, that is, we put the corresponding Haar integrals over each edge, and all the faces also take the Haar integrals.
The ground state
|\Psi_{GS}\rangle
is therefore given by

|\Psi_{C(\Sigma_{1}),C(\Sigma_{d}),C(\Sigma_{2})}(\{h_{H_{1},e_{1}}\},\{\varphi_{\hat{H}_{1},f_{1}}\};\{\varphi_{J_{1},f_{d,1}}\};\{h_{W,e_{d}}\};\{\varphi_{J_{2},f_{d,2}}\};\{h_{H_{2},e_{2}}\},\{\varphi_{\hat{H}_{2},f_{2}}\})\rangle.

The generalization to
N
-bulk domain wall is straightforward. Notice that these states are local quasi-product states
Jia et al. 2020
, thus they satisfy the entanglement area law.
Recently, it is discovered (in group algebra case) that the entanglement entropy of the quantum double phase is sensitive to the existence of the boundary and boundary types
Chen et al. 2018
;
Lou et al. 2019
;
Shen et al. 2019
;
Hu and Wan 2019
(and domain walls
Brown et al. 2013
;
Shi and Kim 2021
).
Using this explicit ground state, we can also analyze the entanglement feature of the extended generalized quantum double model in more detail; this will be done in our future work.

6
Conclusion and discussion

In this work, we establish the theory of gapped boundary and domain wall for the generalized quantum double model.
A gapped boundary is equivalently characterized by an
H
-comodule algebra or an
H
-module algebra.
A domain wall is characterized by an
H_{1}|H_{2}
-bicomodule algebra.
We present the lattice realization and the ground states of these extended quantum double models are solved by using the Hopf tensor network representations of the quantum states.
The boundary and wall excitations and ribbon operators are also discussed in detail.

While significant advancements have been achieved in constructing the gapped boundary and domain wall of the generalized quantum double model using Hamiltonian methods, there are still numerous areas that require further exploration to enhance our comprehension of the theory:

(i) We have successfully constructed the gapped boundary model for a general
H
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
, demonstrating that the boundary local algebra provides the desired boundary phase. However, further investigation is needed to develop a general construction of ribbon operators that realize anyon condensation.

(ii) We have mentioned that the entanglement features of the generalized quantum double model are sensitive to the existence of types of gapped boundaries and domain walls; this part is left for our future study.

(iii) Another interesting aspect is the symmetry-enriched case; some simple cases (cyclic group, Abelian group, etc.) are considered in Refs.
Barkeshli et al. 2019
;
Barkeshli and Cheng 2020
;
Barkeshli et al. 2020
;
Williamson et al. 2017
;
Wang and Cheng 2021
;
Jia and Kaszlikowski 2022
;
Heinrich et al. 2016
. However, the general Hopf algebra case is still largely left open.

(iv) The study of higher dimensional Hopf quantum double models holds significant importance in the investigation of higher dimensional topological order. While the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
group case has been extensively explored, particularly in relation to topological defects characterized by higher categories
Levin and Wen 2005
;
Hamma et al. 2005
;
Kong et al. 2020
;
Delcamp and Schuch 2021
, the more general group cases and Hopf algebra cases still largely remain open topics for research. Further investigations are needed to deepen our understanding of these models and their associated higher-dimensional topological phenomena.

These topics will be covered in our future studies.

Appendix A
C^{*}
Hopf algebra

In this appendix, we will introduce the input data of our construction of lattice realization of
2d
topological ordered phase, that is, a finite-dimensional
C^{*}
Hopf algebra and module coalgebra over it.
And we will collect some properties of them that will be used in this paper. All vector spaces are over the field of complex numbers
\mathbb{C}
.

Definition 13
.

A (complex) Hopf algebra is a complex vector space
H
equipped with several structure morphisms: multiplication
\mu:H\otimes H\to H
, unit
\eta:\mathbb{C}\to H
, comultiplication
\Delta:H\to H\otimes H
, counit
\varepsilon:H\to\mathbb{C}
and antipode
S:H\to H
, for which some consistency conditions are satisfied:

1.

(H,\mu,\eta)
is an algebra:
\mu\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mu\otimes\mathrm{id})=\mu\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mu)
, and
\mu\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\eta\otimes\mathrm{id})=\mathrm{id}=\mu\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}\otimes\eta)
.

2.

(H,\Delta,\varepsilon)
is a coalgebra:
(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta
, and

(\varepsilon\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta=\mathrm{id}=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\varepsilon)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta
.

3.

(H,\mu,\eta,\Delta,\varepsilon)
is a bialgebra:
\Delta
and
\varepsilon
are algebra homomorphisms (equivalently
\mu
and
\eta
are coalgebra homomorphisms).

4.

The antipode
S
satisfies:
\mu\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(S\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta=\eta\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\varepsilon=\mu\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}\otimes S)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta
.

We will use the notation
\mu(x\otimes y)=xy
and
\eta(1)=1_{H}
for multiplication and unit. We will also adopt the Sweedler’s notation
\Delta(u)=\sum_{(u)}u^{(1)}\otimes u^{(2)}:=\sum_{i}u_{i}^{(1)}\otimes u_{i}^{(2)}
.
The comultiplication law ensures that
(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta(u)=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta(u)=\sum_{(u)}u^{(1)}\otimes u^{(2)}\otimes u^{(3)}
. In general, we define
\Delta_{1}=\Delta
and
\Delta_{n}=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta_{n-1}
, then
\Delta_{n}(u)=\sum_{(u)}u^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes u^{(n+1)}
. From the definition above, we have the following useful identity:

\displaystyle\sum_{(x)}\varepsilon(x^{(1)})x^{(2)}=x

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x)}x^{(1)}\varepsilon(x^{(2)}).

(149)

The swap operation is defined as
\tau(x\otimes y)=y\otimes x
, by which one denotes
\mu^{\rm op}=\mu\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\tau
and
\Delta^{\rm op}=\tau\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta
. The opposite Hopf algebra
H^{\rm op}
is defined as
(H,\mu^{\rm op},\eta,\Delta,\varepsilon,S^{-1})
, and the coopposite weak Hopf algebra
H^{\rm cop}
is defined as
(H,\mu,\eta,\Delta^{\rm op},\varepsilon,S^{-1})
.

A Hopf algebra
H
is called
simple
(or
irreducible
) if its algebra
(H,\mu,\eta)
does not have nontrivial ideal, and it is called
semisimple
if its algebra can be written as a direct sum of simple algebras.
From the Larson-Radford theorem,
H
is semisimple if and only if
S^{2}=\mathrm{id}
(also equivalent to the simplicity of the dual Hopf algebra
\hat{H}
).
A
*
-Hopf algebra
(H,*)
is a Hopf algebra
H
equipped with a star structure
*:H\to H
such that
\Delta
is a
*
-homomorphism. That is

(x^{*})^{*}=x,(x+y)^{*}=x^{*}+y^{*},(cx)^{*}=\bar{c}x^{*},(xy)^{*}=y^{*}x^{*},\Delta(x)^{*}=\Delta(x^{*}),

(150)

for all
x,y\in H
and
c\in\mathbb{C}
.
(H,*)
is called a
C^{*}
Hopf algebra if there exists a faithful
*
-representation
\rho:H\to\mathbf{B}(\mathcal{H})
for some operator space over a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Note that a finite-dimensional
C^{*}
Hopf algebra is semisimple.
It can be proved that

S(S(x^{*})^{*})=x.

(151)

In particular, for semisimple Hopf algebra, this implies that
S(x^{*})=S(x)^{*}
.

For a finite-dimensional
C^{*}
Hopf algebra, the antipode satisfies the following properties:

\displaystyle S(xy)=S(y)S(x),\quad S(1_{H})

\displaystyle=1_{H},\quad S^{2}=\mathrm{id},\quad\varepsilon\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}S=\varepsilon,

(152)

\displaystyle\sum_{(S(x))}S(x)^{(1)}\otimes S(x)^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x)}S(x^{(2)})\otimes S(x^{(1)}),

(153)

\displaystyle\sum_{(x)}x^{(1)}S(x^{(2)})=\varepsilon(

\displaystyle x)1_{H}=\sum_{(x)}S(x^{(1)})x^{(2)}.

(154)

These properties are useful for our calculation. Note it follows that
S:H^{\rm op}\to H^{\rm cop}
is an isomorphism of Hopf algebras.

Another notion we will use is the
Haar integral

h\in H
, which is defined as a normalized two-sided integral.
A left (resp. right) integral of
H
is an element
\ell
(resp.
r
) satisfying
x\ell=\varepsilon(x)\ell
(resp.
rx=r\varepsilon(x)
) for all
x\in H
.
If
h
is simultaneously left and right integral, it is called a two-sided integral.
A left (resp. right) integral
\ell
(resp.
r
) is called normalized if
\varepsilon(\ell)=1
(resp.
\varepsilon(r)=1
). We see that normalized integral is idempotent
\ell^{2}=\varepsilon(\ell)\ell=\ell
(resp.
r^{2}=r\varepsilon(r)=r
).
A Haar integral of
H
is a normalized two-sided integral.
Notice that a Haar integral, if exists, must be unique. To see this, suppose that
h,h^{\prime}
are two Haar integrals, then
h^{\prime}=\varepsilon(h)h^{\prime}=hh^{\prime}=h\varepsilon(h^{\prime})=h
. It is easy to see that
S(h)
is a Haar integral if
h
is, thus from uniqueness we see that the Haar integral is
S
-invariant, i.e.
S(h)=h
.
A
C^{*}
Hopf algebra always has a unique Haar integral
h
which satisfies
h^{*}=h
,
h^{2}=h
and
S(h)=h
.
An element
x\in H
is called cocommutative if
\Delta^{\rm op}(x)=\Delta(x)
; the set of all cocommutative elements in
H
is denoted as
\operatorname{Cocom}(H)
. It can be proved that the Haar integral
h
is always cocommutative
\Delta(h)=\Delta^{\rm op}(h)
.

For a given finite-dimensional
C^{*}
Hopf algebra
H
, its dual space
\hat{H}:=\mathrm{Hom}(H,\mathbb{C})=H^{\vee}
has a canonical finite-dimensional
C^{*}
Hopf algebra structure induced by the canonical pairing
\langle\bullet,\bullet\rangle:\hat{H}\times H\to\mathbb{C}
,
\langle\varphi,h\rangle:=\varphi(h)
. More precisely,

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\mu}(\varphi\otimes\psi),x\rangle=(\varphi\otimes\psi)(\Delta(x)),

(155)

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\eta}(1),x\rangle=\varepsilon(x),\hat{1}=\varepsilon,

(156)

\displaystyle\hat{\Delta}(\varphi)(x\otimes y)=\langle\varphi,\mu(x\otimes y)\rangle,

(157)

\displaystyle\hat{\varepsilon}(\varphi)=\langle\varphi,\eta(1)\rangle=\varphi(1_{H}),

(158)

\displaystyle\langle\hat{S}(\varphi),x\rangle=\langle\varphi,S(x)\rangle.

(159)

The star operation on
\hat{H}
is defined as

\langle\varphi^{*},x\rangle=\overline{\langle\varphi,S(x)^{*}\rangle}.

(160)

It is easily checked that
(H^{\rm op})^{\vee}\cong(H^{\vee})^{\rm cop}
and
(H^{\rm cop})^{\vee}\cong(H^{\vee})^{\rm op}
as Hopf algebras.
If
H
is a
C^{*}
Hopf algebra, then there exists a unique Haar integral
h
.
The pairing
\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle:=\langle\varphi^{*}\psi,h\rangle=:\int_{h}\varphi^{*}\psi
for
\varphi,\psi\in\hat{H}
, is an inner product making
\hat{H}
a Hilbert space.
Since
\hat{H}
is also a
C^{*}
Hopf algebra, there also exists a unique Haar integral
\varphi
which induces a Hilbert space structure on
H
,

\langle x,y\rangle_{H}:=\langle\varphi,x^{*}y\rangle

(161)

The Haar integral of
\hat{H}
is also called the Haar measure on
H
.

The quantum double of
H
is the vector space
(H^{\vee})^{\rm cop}\otimes H
equipped with a Hopf algebra structure
10
10

10

There are several different constructions of the quantum double, see
Majid 2000
. Here we choose the one built from
(H^{\vee})^{\rm cop}\otimes H
. See also Sec.
D
for more general construction.
; we denote it as
D(H)=(H^{\vee})^{\rm cop}\Join H
. The multiplication is given by

(\varphi\otimes x)(\psi\otimes y):=\sum_{(x)}\varphi\psi(S^{-1}(x^{(3)})\bullet x^{(1)})\otimes x^{(2)}y,

(162)

where “
\bullet
” denotes the argument of the function.
The other data are given by

\displaystyle 1_{D(H)}=\hat{1}\otimes 1,

(163)

\displaystyle\Delta_{D(H)}(\varphi\otimes x)=\sum_{(\varphi)}\sum_{(x)}(\varphi^{(2)}\otimes x^{(1)})\otimes(\varphi^{(1)}\otimes x^{(2)}),

(164)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{D(H)}(\varphi\otimes x)=\varepsilon(x)\varphi(1_{H}),

(165)

\displaystyle S_{D(H)}(\varphi\otimes x)=\sum_{(\varphi)}\sum_{(x)}\langle\varphi^{(1)}\otimes\varphi^{(3)},h^{(3)}\otimes S^{-1}(h^{(1)})\rangle\hat{S}^{-1}(\varphi^{(2)})\otimes S(h^{(2)}).

(166)

From expression of antipode, we see that the antipode of
D(H)
is involutive if and only if the antipode of
H
is involutive. Then using the Larson-Radford theorem,
D(H)
is semisimple if and only if
H
is semisimple.
Both
(H^{\vee})^{\rm cop},H
can be embedded in
D(H)
as Hopf subalgebras by
\varphi\mapsto\varphi\otimes 1
and
x\mapsto\hat{1}\otimes x
, respectively.
For more details, the reader can consult Refs.
Majid 2000
;
Drinfel’d 1988
;
Kassel 1995
;
Abe 2004
. In particular, we have the following useful straightening formula

x\varphi=\sum_{(x)}\varphi(S^{-1}(x^{(3)})\bullet x^{(1)})x^{(2)},

(167)

which will be useful in dealing with computation involving ribbon operators.

The above is the input data for the bulk. For boundaries and domain walls, we need to introduce the notion of
H
-comodule algebra.

Definition 14
.

Let
H
be a Hopf algebra and
M
a vector space. If there is a linear map
\beta_{M}:M\to H\otimes M
such that

(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\beta_{M}=(\mathrm{id}_{H}\otimes\beta_{M})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\beta_{M},\quad(\varepsilon\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\beta_{M}=\mathrm{id}_{M},

(168)

then
M
is called a left
H
-comodule, and
\beta_{M}
is called a left coaction.
The right
H
-comodule can be defined similarly.

Definition 15
.

Let
H
be a Hopf algebra and
A
an algebra. If
A
is an
H
-module such that
h\triangleright(xy)=\sum_{(h)}(h^{(1)}\triangleright x)(h^{(2)}\triangleright y)
and
h\triangleright 1_{A}=\varepsilon(h)1_{A}
, then
A
is called an
H
-module algebra.

Definition 16
.

Let
H
be a Hopf algebra and
A
an algebra. If
A
is a left
H
-comodule with left coaction
\beta_{A}:A\to H\otimes A
such that
\beta_{A}(xy)=\beta_{A}(x)\beta_{A}(y)
and
\beta(1_{A})=1_{H}\otimes 1_{A}
, then
A
is called a left
H
-comodule algebra. The right
H
-comodule algebra can be defined similarly.

Theorem 1

(See
Andruskiewitsch and Mombelli 2007
)
.

The indecomposable exact module categories over the UFC
\mathsf{Rep}(H)
of representations of a finite-dimensional Hopf algebra
H
are classified by
H
-comodule algebras up to Morita equivalence.

Definition 17

(Smash product)
.

Let
H
be a Hopf algebra and
A
a left
H
-module algebra. The smash product algebra
A\#H
is defined as follows.

1.

As a vector space
A\#H=A\otimes H
; we denote
a\#h
for element
a\otimes h
.

2.

The product is given by

(a\#h)(b\#g)=\sum_{(h)}a(h^{(1)}b)\#h^{(2)}g,

(169)

and the identity is
1\#1
.

When
\mathfrak{M}
is a left
H
-module algebra,
\mathfrak{M}^{\rm op}
is a left
H^{\rm cop}
-module algebra.
The smash product
\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{M}^{\rm op}\#H^{\rm cop}
is a left
H
-comodule algebra with coaction given by

\beta_{\mathfrak{A}}(a\#h)=\sum_{(h)}h^{(1)}\otimes(a\#h^{(2)}),

(170)

where the comultiplication label is taken in
H
.

Definition 18
.

A Frobenius algebra is a quintuple
(\mathfrak{F},\mu,\eta,\Delta,\varepsilon)
with
\mu:\mathfrak{F}\otimes\mathfrak{F}\to\mathfrak{F}
,
\eta:\mathbb{C}\to\mathfrak{F}
,
\Delta:\mathfrak{F}\to\mathfrak{F}\otimes\mathfrak{F}
and
\varepsilon:\mathfrak{F}\to\mathbb{C}
, such that
(\mathfrak{F},\mu,\eta)
forms an algebra and
(\mathfrak{F},\Delta,\varepsilon)
forms a coalgebra and

(\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{F}}\otimes\mu)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{F}})=\Delta\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\mu=(\mu\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{F}})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{F}}\otimes\Delta).

(171)

A Frobenius algebra is called special if there exist
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
such that

\mu\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta=\alpha\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{F}},\quad\varepsilon\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\eta=\beta\mathrm{id}_{\mathds{1}}.

(172)

When
\alpha=1
and
\beta=\dim\mathfrak{F}
, it is said normalized-special (or separable).
A Frobenius algebra is said symmetric if

(\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{F}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{F}^{\vee}})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mu_{\mathfrak{F}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{F}^{\vee}})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{F}}\otimes\operatorname{coev}_{\mathfrak{F}})=(\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{F}^{\vee}}\otimes\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{F}})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{F}^{\vee}}\otimes\mu_{\mathfrak{F}})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\operatorname{coev}_{\mathfrak{F}^{\vee}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{F}}),

(173)

where
\operatorname{coev}_{\mathfrak{F}}
is the coevaluation map.

Appendix B
Geometric objects on
2d
lattice

In this appendix, following Ref.
Bombin and Martin-Delgado 2008
, we provide a detailed discussion of geometric objects that appeared in the quantum double model.

Definition 19

(Lattice on a surface)
.

Let
\Sigma
be a
2d
surface, a lattice
C(\Sigma)
on it is a simple graph embedded in
\Sigma
, where, by simple graph we mean a graph for which no edge starts and ends at the same vertex.

It can be proved that, for an arbitrary
2d
surface, this kind of lattice embedding always exists. Another way to understand the lattice is from the perspective of cellulation, and the result coincides with the graph embedding.
A typical example is a triangulation, which always exists for topological manifolds with dimensions less than or equal to three
Manolescu 2016
.
A slightly more complicated case is to consider the lattice as a ribbon graph embedded in the surface
Meusburger 2017
;
Meusburger and Wise 2021
, but there is no big difference. We can always break the ribbon graph into a simple graph by breaking edges and adding some new edges.
Notice that for a given surface, different lattices on it have the same topological invariant (e.g., the same Euler characteristics), thus they are topologically equivalent.
For the surface with boundaries, similar results hold.
We first make a graph embedding of the boundary, which is nothing but an
m
-vertex cycle. The bulk graph shares these vertices and edges on the boundary.

Definition 20
.

For a lattice
C(\Sigma)
on the surface
\Sigma
, we denote its vertex set, edge set, and face set as
V(\Sigma)
,
E(\Sigma)
and
F(\Sigma)
respectively.
The dual lattice
\tilde{C}(\Sigma)
is the Poincaré dual of
C(\Sigma)
, for which the role of face set and vertex set interchange.
We also introduce a direction of each edge
e\in E(\Sigma)
, and the direction of the dual edge is obtained by rotating the direct edge counterclockwise with
\pi/2
. The inverse edge
\tilde{e}
is obtained by reversing the direction of edge
e
.
Two ends of edge
e
are denoted as
\partial_{i}e
with
i=0,1
indicating the starting and terminal ends.
A direct path
p
is a list
p=(v_{0},e_{1},\cdots,e_{n},v_{n})
for which
\partial_{0}e_{k}=v_{k-1}
and
\partial e_{k}=v_{k}
. The dual path is thus a list
\tilde{p}=(f_{0},\tilde{e}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{e}_{n},f_{n})
such that
\partial_{0}e_{k}=f_{k-1}
and
\partial e_{k}=f_{k}
.

Definition 21
.

A site is a pair
s=(v,f)
with adjacent
v\in V(\Sigma)
and
f\in F(\Sigma)
.
A direct triangle
\tau=(s_{0},s_{1},e)
consists of two sites
s_{0},s_{1}
and a direct edge
e
, for which
s_{0}
and
s_{1}
share a common face. A dual triangle
\tilde{\tau}=(s_{0},s_{1},\tilde{e})
consists of two sites
s_{0},s_{1}
and a dual edge
\tilde{e}
, for which
s_{0}
and
s_{1}
share a common vertex. The two sites of the triangle are denoted as
\partial_{i}\tau
with
i=0,1
indicating the starting and terminal ends.
Notice that each edge has its direction, which may or may not match the direction of the triangle.
A left-handed (right-handed) triangle is one for which the edge is on the left-hand (right-hand) side when we pass through the triangle along its positive direction.
Two triangles overlap if there share some common area. Two direct (dual) triangles overlap only if they are the same triangle. A direct triangle overlaps with a dual triangle when their direct edge and dual edge coincide.

Definition 22
.

A strip is an alternating sequence of triangles
\rho=(\tau_{1},\cdots,\tau_{n})
such that
\partial_{1}\tau_{k}=\partial_{0}\tau_{k+1}
. The starting and terminal site are denoted as
\partial_{0}\rho=\partial_{0}\tau_{1}
and
\partial_{1}\rho=\partial_{1}\tau_{n}
.
Notice that a strip may have self-overlapping.
A strip without self-overlapping is called a ribbon.

•

A strip (ribbon) is called empty if its length is zero (no triangle).

•

A strip (ribbon) is called direct (dual) if it consists only of direct (dual) triangles.

•

A strip (ribbon) is called proper if it contains both direct and dual triangles. For a proper strip (ribbon), there is an associated direct path
p_{\rho}
and a dual path
\tilde{p}_{\rho}
.

•

A strip (ribbon) is called closed if
\partial_{0}\rho=\partial_{1}\rho
,
viz.
, its starting site and terminal site are the same site. The only end site of the closed strip (ribbon) is denoted as
\partial\rho
.

•

A strip (ribbon) is called open if

\partial_{0}\rho
and
\partial_{1}\rho
have no overlap (
v_{\partial_{0}\rho}\neq v_{\partial_{1}\rho}
and
v_{\partial_{0}\rho}\neq v_{\partial_{1}\rho}
).

Definition 23
.

A directed strip (ribbon) is called type-A (resp. type-B) if all the direct triangles in it are left-handed (resp. right-handed).

Definition 24
.

A direct closed ribbon is a closed ribbon consisting of only direct triangles. A dual closed ribbon is a closed ribbon consisting of only dual triangles.

Appendix C
Properties of ribbon operators

In this appendix, we provide some detailed discussion and calculations of ribbon operators.

C.1
Triangle operators

The building blocks for the ribbon operators are eight triangle operators given in Eqs. (
17
)-(
24
).
We see that they are determined by the left-handed edge operators
T_{\pm}
,
L_{\pm}
, and right-handed edge operators
\tilde{T}_{\pm}
and
\tilde{L}_{\pm}
.
Recall that the inner product on the Hopf algebra
H
is given by
\langle x,y\rangle=\varphi_{\hat{H}}(x^{*}y)
, where
\varphi_{\hat{H}}
is the Haar integral of
\hat{H}
.

The left-handed operator and right-handed operator are independent, and they won’t appear in a given ribbon simultaneously. Only when considering the overlap of two ribbon operators with different chiralities, need we be concerned with the relation between them.

It is easy to verify that the left-handed operators satisfy

L^{h}_{-}=S\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}L_{+}^{h}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}S^{-1},\quad T_{-}^{\phi}=S^{-1}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}T_{+}^{\phi}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}S.

(174)

These edge operators are also compatible with
*
-structures of
H
and
\hat{H}
:

\displaystyle S^{\dagger}=S,\quad(L_{\pm}^{h})^{\dagger}=L_{\pm}^{h^{*}},\quad(T_{\pm}^{\varphi})^{\dagger}=T_{\pm}^{\varphi^{*}}.

(175)

For completeness, here we give proof of Eq. (
175
).
First consider
\langle S^{\dagger}(x),y\rangle=\varphi_{\hat{H}}(x^{*}S(y))=S(\varphi_{\hat{H}})(S(x^{*}S(y))=\varphi_{\hat{H}}(S(x^{*})y)=\langle S(x),y\rangle
, thus
S^{\dagger}=S
.
The proof of
(L^{h}_{\pm})^{\dagger}=L_{\pm}^{h^{*}}
is almost straightforward (see Sec. III B of
Buerschaper et al. 2013a
for a detailed proof). We will prove the
T_{\pm}^{\phi}
part, which is relatively technical. Namely, we have

\displaystyle\langle x,T^{\phi}_{+}y\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y)}\varphi_{\hat{H}}(x^{*}y^{(1)}\phi(y^{(2)}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y),(\phi),(x^{*})}\varphi_{\hat{H}}({x^{*}}^{(1)}\varepsilon({x^{*}}^{(2)})\phi^{(2)}(1_{H})\phi^{(1)}(y^{(2)})y^{(1)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y),(\phi),(x^{*})}\varphi_{\hat{H}}({x^{*}}^{(1)}y^{(1)}1_{\hat{H}}({x^{*}}^{(2)})\hat{\varepsilon}(\phi^{(2)})\phi^{(1)}(y^{(2)}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y),(\phi),(x^{*})}\varphi_{\hat{H}}({x^{*}}^{(1)}y^{(1)}[\phi^{(2)}S(\phi^{(3)})]({x^{*}}^{(2)})\phi^{(1)}(y^{(2)}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y),(\phi),(x^{*})}\varphi_{\hat{H}}({x^{*}}^{(1)}y^{(1)}\phi^{(2)}({x^{*}}^{(2)})S(\phi^{(3)})({x^{*}}^{(3)})\phi^{(1)}(y^{(2)}))

(176)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y),(\phi),(x^{*})}\varphi_{\hat{H}}((y{x^{*}}^{(1)})^{(1)}\phi^{(1)}((y{x^{*}}^{(1)})^{(2)})S(\phi^{(2)})({x^{*}}^{(2)}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y),(\phi),(x^{*})}\varphi_{\hat{H}}((y{x^{*}}^{(1)})^{(1)}\phi^{(1)}((y{x^{*}}^{(1)})^{(2)})S(\phi^{(2)})({x^{*}}^{(2)}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\phi),(x^{*})}\varphi_{\hat{H}}({x^{*}}^{(1)}y\hat{\varepsilon}(\phi^{(1)})S(\phi^{(2)})({x^{*}}^{(2)}))

\displaystyle=\langle T^{\phi^{*}}_{+}x,y\rangle,

where we have used
\varphi_{\hat{H}}\phi^{(1)}=\hat{\varepsilon}(\phi^{(1)})\varphi_{\hat{H}}
and Eq. (
160
). Using Eq. (
174
), we have
(T^{\phi}_{-})^{\dagger}=S^{\dagger}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}T^{\phi^{*}}_{+}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}S^{\dagger}=T^{\phi^{*}}_{-}
.

Using the definition of these operators, it is not difficult to work out the commutation relations among these operators,

\displaystyle T^{\varphi}_{+}L_{+}^{h}=\sum_{(h)}L_{+}^{h^{(1)}}T_{+}^{\varphi(h^{(2)}\bullet)},\quad T^{\varphi}_{+}L_{-}^{h}=\sum_{(S(h))}L_{-}^{S(h)^{(1)}}T_{+}^{\varphi(\bullet S(h)^{(2)})},

(177)

\displaystyle T_{-}^{\varphi}L_{+}^{h}=\sum_{(h)}L_{+}^{h^{(2)}}T_{-}^{\varphi(\bullet S(h^{(1)}))},\quad T_{-}^{\varphi}L_{-}^{h}=\sum_{(h)}L_{-}^{h^{(2)}}T_{-}^{\varphi(h^{(2)}\bullet)},

(178)

where “
\bullet
” denotes the argument of the function.

The right-handed edge operators and left-handed edge operators are related via antipode by

\tilde{L}_{\pm}^{h}=L^{S(h)}_{\mp},\quad\tilde{T}^{\phi}_{\pm}=T^{\hat{S}(\phi)}_{\mp}.

(179)

Thus from the properties of left-handed edge operators, we similarly have:

\tilde{L}^{h}_{+}=S\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\tilde{L}_{-}^{h}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}S^{-1},\quad\tilde{T}_{+}^{\phi}=S\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\tilde{T}_{-}^{\phi}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}S^{-1},

(180)

and

\displaystyle(\tilde{L}_{\pm}^{h})^{\dagger}=\tilde{L}_{\pm}^{h^{*}},\quad(\tilde{T}_{\pm}^{\varphi})^{\dagger}=\tilde{T}_{\pm}^{\varphi^{*}}.

(181)

The commutation relations for right-handed edge operators (and the commutation relations between left-handed and right-handed edge operators) can also be obtained from Eqs. (
179
), (
177
) and (
178
).

It is easy to prove that

\displaystyle L^{h}_{\pm}L^{g}_{\pm}=L^{hg}_{\pm},\quad T_{\pm}^{\phi}T_{\pm}^{\psi}=T_{\pm}^{\phi\psi},

(182)

\displaystyle\tilde{L}^{h}_{\pm}\tilde{L}^{g}_{\pm}=\tilde{L}^{gh}_{\pm},\quad\tilde{T}^{\phi}_{\pm}T^{\psi}_{\pm}=\tilde{T}^{\psi\phi}_{\pm}.

From the above equalities and the definition of triangle operators, it is clear that

\displaystyle F^{h,\phi}(\tau_{L})F^{g,\psi}(\tau_{L})=F^{hg,\psi\phi}(\tau_{L}),\quad F^{h,\phi}(\tilde{\tau}_{R})F^{g,\psi}(\tilde{\tau}_{R})=F^{hg,\psi\phi}(\tilde{\tau}_{R}),

(183)

\displaystyle F^{h,\phi}(\tau_{R})F^{g,\psi}(\tau_{R})=F^{gh,\phi\psi}(\tau_{R}),\quad F^{h,\phi}(\tilde{\tau}_{L})F^{g,\psi}(\tilde{\tau}_{L})=F^{gh,\phi\psi}(\tilde{\tau}_{L}),

(184)

This means that, as algebras, the triangle operator algebras satisfy
\mathcal{A}_{\tau_{L}}\cong\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{\tau}_{R}}\cong H\otimes\hat{H}^{\rm op}
, and
\mathcal{A}_{\tau_{R}}\cong\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{\tau}_{L}}\cong H^{\rm op}\otimes\hat{H}
.

C.2
Vertex and face operators

The type-B construction of the quantum double model is based on the left-module structure of
H
.
The vertex operators
{A}^{h}(s)
are built from
L_{\pm}
in counterclockwise order, and the face operators
{B}^{\varphi}(s)
are built from
T_{\pm}
in a counterclockwise order.
It is easy to verify that

{A}^{h}(s){B}^{\varphi}(s)=\sum_{(h)}{B}^{\varphi(S^{-1}(h^{(3)})\bullet h^{(1)})}(s){A}^{h^{(2)}}(s).

(185)

See Sec. III A in Ref.
Buerschaper et al. 2013a
. They form a representation of
D_{B}(H)=\hat{H}^{\rm cop}\Join H
.

The type-A construction of the quantum double model is based on the right-module structure of
H
.
The vertex operators
\tilde{A}^{h}(s)
are built from
\tilde{L}_{\pm}
in a clockwise order, and the face operators
\tilde{B}^{\varphi}(s)
are built from
\tilde{T}_{\pm}
in a clockwise order.
It is easy to verify that

\tilde{A}^{h}(s)\tilde{B}^{\varphi}(s)=\sum_{(h)}\tilde{B}^{\varphi(h^{(3)}\bullet S^{-1}(h^{(1)}))}(s)\tilde{A}^{h^{(2)}}(s).

(186)

They form a representation of
D_{A}(H)=\hat{H}\Join H^{\rm cop}\simeq D_{B}(H)^{\rm cop}
.

C.3
Properties of ribbon operators

There are several crucial properties for open ribbon operators that will be used for constructing topological excitations of the model.

As we have mentioned before, the topological excitations are given at two ends of the ribbon operators, thus the commutation relations between the vertex and face operators and ribbon operators are crucial:

1.

At the starting points of ribbons
\rho_{A}
and
\rho_{B}
, we have

\displaystyle A^{g}(s_{0})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho_{A})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}F^{g^{(1)}hS(g^{(3)}),\varphi(S(g^{(2)})\bullet)}(\rho_{A})A^{g^{(4)}}(s_{0}),

(187)

\displaystyle A^{g}(s_{0})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho_{B})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}F^{g^{(2)}hS(g^{(4)}),\varphi(S(g^{(3)})\bullet)}(\rho_{B})A^{g^{(1)}}(s_{0}),

(188)

\displaystyle B^{\psi}(s_{0})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho_{A})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}F^{h^{(2)},\varphi}(\rho_{A})B^{\psi(\bullet S(h^{(1)}))}(s_{0}),

(189)

\displaystyle B^{\psi}(s_{0})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho_{B})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}F^{h^{(2)},\varphi}(\rho_{B})B^{\psi(S(h^{(1)})\bullet)}(s_{0}).

(190)

2.

Similarly, at the ending points, we have

\displaystyle A^{g}(s_{1})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho_{A})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}F^{h,\varphi(\bullet g^{(2)})}(\rho_{A})A^{g^{(1)}}(s_{1}),

(191)

\displaystyle A^{g}(s_{1})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho_{B})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}F^{h,\varphi(\bullet g^{(1)})}(\rho_{B})A^{g^{(2)}}(s_{1}),

(192)

\displaystyle B^{\psi}(s_{1})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho_{A})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\sum_{k,(k)}\varphi(k^{(2)})F^{h^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{A})B^{\psi(S(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)}\bullet)}(s_{1}),

(193)

\displaystyle B^{\psi}(s_{1})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho_{B})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\sum_{k,(k)}\varphi(k^{(2)})F^{h^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{B})B^{\psi(\bullet S(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)})}(s_{1}).

(194)

See
Yan et al. 2022
for detailed proofs (although the convention we use here is different from the one in
Yan et al. 2022
, but the same result can be obtained).

Proposition 10
.

Let
\rho
be a closed ribbon with end site
s=\partial\rho
.

1.

If
\rho=\rho_{A}
is of type-A, we have

\displaystyle A^{h}(s)F^{g,\psi}(\rho_{A})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}F^{h^{(1)}gS(h^{(3)}),\psi(S(h^{(2)})\bullet h^{(5)})}(\rho_{A})A^{h^{(4)}}(s),

(195)

\displaystyle B^{\varphi}(s)F^{g,\psi}(\rho_{A})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}F^{g^{(2)},\psi}(\rho_{A})B^{\varphi(g^{(3)}\bullet S(g^{(1)}))}(s).

(196)

2.

If
\rho=\rho_{B}
is of type-B, we have

\displaystyle A^{h}(s)F^{g,\psi}(\rho_{B})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}F^{h^{(3)}gS(h^{(5)}),\psi(S(h^{(4)})\bullet h^{(1)})}(\rho_{B})A^{h^{(2)}}(s),

(197)

\displaystyle B^{\varphi}(s)F^{g,\psi}(\rho_{B})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}F^{g^{(2)},\psi}(\rho_{B})B^{\varphi(S(g^{(1)})\bullet g^{(3)})}(s).

(198)

Proof.

Decompose
\rho
into
\rho=\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}
with
\partial_{1}\rho_{1}=\partial_{0}\rho_{2}
and
\partial_{0}\rho_{1}=\partial_{1}\rho_{2}=s
. Assume
\rho
is of type-B first. Using Eqs. (
188
) and (
192
), we have

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ A^{h}(s)F^{g,\psi}(\rho_{B})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(g)}A^{h}(s)F^{g^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{S(k^{(3)})g^{(2)}k^{(1)},\psi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(g)}\sum_{(h)}F^{h^{(2)}g^{(1)}S(h^{(4)}),\hat{k}(S(h^{(3)})\bullet)}(\rho_{1})A^{h^{(1)}}(s)F^{S(k^{(3)})g^{(2)}k^{(1)},\psi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k(k),(g)}\sum_{(h)}F^{h^{(3)}g^{(1)}S(h^{(5)}),\hat{k}(S(h^{(4)})\bullet)}(\rho_{1})F^{S(k^{(3)})g^{(2)}k^{(1)},\psi(k^{(2)}\bullet h^{(1)})}(\rho_{2})A^{h^{(2)}}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(g)}\sum_{(h)}\sum_{j}F^{h^{(3)}g^{(1)}S(h^{(5)}),\hat{k}(S(h^{(4)})j)\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S(k^{(3)})g^{(2)}k^{(1)},\psi(k^{(2)}\bullet h^{(1)})}(\rho_{2})A^{h^{(2)}}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}\sum_{j,(j),(g)}F^{h^{(3)}g^{(1)}S(h^{(7)}),\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S(j^{(3)})h^{(4)}g^{(2)}S(h^{(6)})j^{(1)},\psi(S(h^{(5)})j^{(2)}\bullet h^{(1)})}(\rho_{2})A^{h^{(2)}}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}F^{h^{(3)}gS(h^{(5)}),\psi(S(h^{(4)})\bullet h^{(1)})}(\rho_{B})A^{h^{(2)}}(s).

(199)

Using straightening formula,
Eq. (
194
) can be rewritten as

B^{\psi}(s_{1})F^{h,\varphi}(\rho_{B})=\sum_{(h)}F^{h^{(1)},\varphi}(\rho_{B})B^{\psi(\bullet h^{(2)})}(s_{1}).

(200)

Thus, by Eqs. (
190
) and the above, we have

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ B^{\varphi}(s)F^{g,\psi}(\rho_{B})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(g)}B^{\varphi}(s)F^{g^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{S(k^{(3)})g^{(2)}k^{(1)},\psi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(g)}F^{g^{(2)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})B^{\varphi(S(g^{(1)})\bullet)}(s)F^{S(k^{(3)})g^{(3)}k^{(1)},\psi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(g)}F^{g^{(2)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{S(k^{(5)})g^{(3)}k^{(1)},\psi(k^{(3)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})B^{\varphi(S(g^{(1)})\bullet S(k^{(4)})g^{(4)}k^{(2)})}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}\sum_{k,(k),(g^{(2)})}F^{g^{(2)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{S(k^{(3)})g^{(3)}k^{(1)},\psi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})B^{\varphi(S(g^{(1)})\bullet g^{(4)})}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}F^{g^{(2)},\psi}(\rho)B^{\varphi(S(g^{(1)})\bullet g^{(3)})}(s).

(201)

Here, in the fourth equality, we apply the straightening formula.
This completes the proof for the assertion of closed ribbons of type-B. The proof for the assertion of closed ribbons of type-A is similar.
∎

Proposition 11
.

The ribbon operator
F^{g,\psi}(\rho)
for an open ribbon
\rho
commutes with all operators
A_{v}^{H}=A^{h_{H}}(s)
and
B^{\hat{H}}_{f}=B^{\varphi_{\hat{H}}}(s)
for
s\neq\partial_{0}\rho,\partial_{1}\rho
, where
h_{H}
and
\varphi_{\hat{H}}
are Haar integrals.

Proof.

We prove the assertion for type-B ribbons. Denote
h=h_{H}
and
\varphi=\varphi_{\hat{H}}
for simplicity. Decompose
\rho
into
\rho=\tau_{1}\cup\tau_{2}
, with
\partial_{1}\tau_{1}=\partial_{0}\tau_{2}=s
:
