Using Eqs. (
188
) and (
192
), one gets

\displaystyle\quad A^{h}(s)F^{g,\psi}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(g)}A^{h}(s)F^{g^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\tau_{1})F^{S(k^{(3)})g^{(2)}k^{(1)},\psi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(g)}\sum_{(h)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{k}(\bullet h^{(1)})}(\tau_{1})A^{h^{(2)}}(s)F^{S(k^{(3)})g^{(2)}k^{(1)},\psi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(g)}\sum_{(h)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{k}(\bullet h^{(1)})}(\tau_{1})F^{h^{(3)}S(k^{(3)})g^{(2)}k^{(1)}S(h^{(5)}),\psi(k^{(2)}S(h^{(4)})\bullet)}(\tau_{2})A^{h^{(2)}}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(g)}\sum_{(h)}\sum_{j}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{k}(jh^{(1)})\hat{j}}(\tau_{1})F^{h^{(3)}S(k^{(3)})g^{(2)}k^{(1)}S(h^{(5)}),\psi(k^{(2)}S(h^{(4)})\bullet)}(\tau_{2})A^{h^{(2)}}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,(j),(g)}\sum_{(h)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\tau_{1})F^{h^{(5)}S(h^{(3)})S(j^{(3)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)}h^{(1)}S(h^{(7)}),\psi(j^{(2)}h^{(2)}S(h^{(6)})\bullet)}(\tau_{2})A^{h^{(4)}}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,(j),(g)}\sum_{(h)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\tau_{1})F^{h^{(4)}S(h^{(2)})S(j^{(3)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)},\psi(j^{(2)}h^{(1)}S(h^{(5)})\bullet)}(\tau_{2})A^{h^{(3)}}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,(j),(g)}\sum_{(h)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\tau_{1})F^{h^{(3)}S(h^{(1)})S(j^{(3)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)},\psi(j^{(2)}\bullet)}(\tau_{2})A^{h^{(2)}}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,(j),(g)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\tau_{1})F^{S(j^{(3)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)},\psi(j^{(2)}\bullet)}(\tau_{2})A^{h}(s)

\displaystyle=F^{g,\psi}(\rho)A^{h}(s).

(202)

Here, in the fourth equality, we use
\bullet=\sum_{j}\hat{j}(\bullet)j
for an orthogonal basis
\{j\}
; in the fifth equality, we use
jh^{(1)}=\sum_{k}\hat{k}(jh^{(1)})k
; in the sixth equality, we use the fact that
h=h_{H}
is cocommutative which enable us to rotate
h^{(7)}
to
h^{(1)}
and
h^{(i)}
to
h^{(i+1)}
for
1\leq i\leq 6
; the seventh and eighth equalities use the same trick as in the sixth.

For the second statement, using Eqs. (
190
) and (
194
), one has

\displaystyle\quad B^{\varphi}(s)F^{g,\psi}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(g)}B^{\varphi}(s)F^{g^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{S(k^{(3)})g^{(2)}k^{(1)},\psi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(g)}\sum_{j,(j)}\hat{k}(j^{(2)})F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})B^{\varphi(\bullet S(j^{(3)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)})}(s)F^{S(k^{(3)})g^{(3)}k^{(1)},\psi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,(k),(g)}\sum_{j,(j)}\hat{k}(j^{(2)})F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S(k^{(4)})g^{(4)}k^{(2)},\psi(k^{(3)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})B^{\varphi(S(k^{(1)})S(g^{(3)})k^{(5)}\bullet S(j^{(3)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)})}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}\sum_{j,(j)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S(j^{(5)})g^{(4)}j^{(3)},\psi(j^{(4)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})B^{\varphi(S(j^{(2)})S(g^{(3)})j^{(6)}\bullet S(j^{(7)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)})}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}\sum_{j,(j)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S(j^{(5)})g^{(4)}j^{(3)},\psi(j^{(4)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})B^{\varphi(S(j^{(7)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)}S(j^{(2)})S(g^{(3)})j^{(6)}\bullet)}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(g)}\sum_{j,(j)}F^{g^{(1)},\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{S(j^{(3)})g^{(2)}j^{(1)},\psi(j^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})B^{\varphi}(s)

\displaystyle=F^{g,\psi}(\rho)B^{\varphi}(s).

(203)

Here, in the fifth equality, we use the fact that
\varphi
is cocommutative.
This completes the proof of the assertion for type-B ribbons. The assertion for type-A ribbons can be proved similarly
Yan et al. 2022
.
∎

Proposition 12
.

The ribbon operator
F^{h,\phi}(\rho)
of a closed ribbon
\rho
commutes with all stabilizers
A_{v}^{H}=A^{h_{H}}(s)
and
B_{f}^{\hat{H}}=B^{\varphi_{H}}(s)
with
s\neq\partial\rho
.

Proof.

Denote
s_{0}=\partial\rho
and
s=(v,f)
. We only need to consider the case that
s
lies on
\rho
. Note that we can always find an
s_{1}
in
\rho
such that
\rho=\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}
with
\partial_{0}\rho_{1}=\partial_{1}\rho_{2}=s_{0},\partial_{1}\rho_{1}=\partial_{0}\rho_{2}=s_{1}
and
s\neq s_{1}
. Then
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
are open ribbons and
s
is not an end site of them. It follows from Proposition
11
that
A^{H}_{v}
and
B_{f}^{\hat{H}}
commute with
F^{(h\otimes\varphi)^{(1)}}(\rho_{1})
and
F^{(h\otimes\varphi)^{(2)}}(\rho_{2})
, and hence with
F^{h,\varphi}(\rho)
by the recursive formula of ribbon operator.
∎

Proof of the commutation relations (
102
-
105
)

We will prove the commutation relations for type-A ribbons. The proof for type-B ribbons is similar.

\bullet
(
102
) for short ribbon:

a_{1}
a_{3}
s_{b}
a_{2}

\displaystyle\quad A^{k}(s_{b})F^{[h],\varphi}(\rho_{A})|a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum A^{k}(s_{1})\varepsilon(h)\varphi(S(a_{3}^{(2)}))|a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varepsilon(h)\varphi(S(a_{3}^{(2)}))|a_{1}S(k^{(1)}),k^{(2)}a_{3},k^{(3)}a_{3}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varepsilon(h)\varphi(S(S(k^{(5)})k^{(4)}a_{3}^{(2)}))|a_{1}S(k^{(1)}),k^{(2)}a_{2},k^{(3)}a_{3}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varepsilon(h)\varphi(S(k^{(4)}a^{(2)}_{3})k^{(5)})|a_{1}S(k^{(1)}),k^{(2)}a_{2},k^{(3)}a_{3}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum F^{[h],\varphi(\bullet k^{(4)})}(\rho)|a_{1}S(k^{(1)}),k^{(2)}a_{2},k^{(3)}a_{3}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum F^{[h],\varphi(\bullet k^{(2)})}(\rho_{A})A^{k^{(1)}}(s_{b})|a_{1},a_{3},a_{3}\rangle.

\bullet
(
104
) for short ribbon:

a_{4}
a_{1}
s_{b}
a_{3}
a_{2}

\displaystyle\quad B^{\psi}(s_{1})F^{[h],\varphi}(\rho)|a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum B^{\psi}(s_{1})F^{[h^{(1)}],\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{[S(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)}],\varphi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})|a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum B^{\psi}(s_{1})\varepsilon(\hat{k})\varepsilon(S(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)})\varphi(k^{(2)}S(a_{1}^{(2)}))|a_{1}^{(1)},h^{(1)}h_{K}a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum B^{\psi}(s_{1})\varphi(S(a_{1}^{(2)}))|a_{1}^{(1)},hh_{K}a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi(S(a_{1}^{(3)}))\psi(a_{1}^{(2)}h^{(2)}h_{K}^{(2)}a_{2}^{(2)}a_{3}^{(2)}a_{4}^{(2)})|a_{1}^{(1)},h^{(1)}h_{K}^{(1)}a_{2}^{(1)},a_{3}^{(1)},a_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi(S(a_{1}^{(3)}))\psi(a_{1}^{(2)}h^{(2)}h_{K}^{(2)}h_{K}a_{2}^{(2)}a_{3}^{(2)}a_{4}^{(2)})|a_{1}^{(1)},h^{(1)}h_{K}^{(1)}a_{2}^{(1)},a_{3}^{(1)},a_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi(S(a_{1}^{(3)}))\psi(a_{1}^{(2)}h^{(2)}a_{2}^{(2)}a_{3}^{(2)}a_{4}^{(2)})|a_{1}^{(1)},h^{(1)}h_{K}a_{2}^{(1)},a_{3}^{(1)},a_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi(S(a_{1}^{(3)}))\psi(a_{1}^{(2)}h^{(2)}S(a_{1}^{(4)})a_{1}^{(5)}a_{2}^{(2)}a_{3}^{(2)}a_{4}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad|a_{1}^{(1)},h^{(1)}h_{K}a_{2}^{(1)},a_{3}^{(1)},a_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi(k^{(2)})\hat{k}(S(a_{1}^{(2)}))\psi(S(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)}a_{1}^{(3)}a_{2}^{(2)}a_{3}^{(2)}a_{4}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad|a_{1}^{(1)},h^{(1)}h_{K}a_{2}^{(1)},a_{3}^{(1)},a_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi(k^{(2)})\hat{k}(j^{(2)}S(a_{1}^{(2)}))\psi(S(k^{(3)})h^{(3)}k^{(1)}a_{1}^{(3)}a_{2}^{(2)}a_{3}^{(2)}a_{4}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\varepsilon(S(j^{(3)}))\varepsilon(h^{(2)})\varepsilon(j^{(1)})\varepsilon(\hat{j})|a_{1}^{(1)},h^{(1)}h_{K}a_{2}^{(1)},a_{3}^{(1)},a_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi(k^{(2)})F^{[h^{(1)}],\hat{j}}(\rho_{1})F^{[S(j^{(3)})h^{(2)}j^{(1)}],\hat{k}(j^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\psi(S(k^{(3)})h^{(3)}k^{(1)}a_{1}^{(3)}a_{2}^{(2)}a_{3}^{(2)}a_{4}^{(2)})|a_{1}^{(1)},a_{2}^{(1)},a_{3}^{(1)},a_{4}^{(1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi(k^{(2)})F^{[h^{(1)}],\hat{k}}(\rho)B^{\psi(S(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)}\bullet)}(s_{1})|a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}\rangle.

Note that for any
h\in H
, since
h=hh_{K}+h(1-h_{K})
and
(1-h_{K})\in K^{+}
,
[h]=[hh_{K}]
in
H/HK^{+}
, and since
\psi\in(H/HK^{+})^{\vee}
only depends on the equivalence classes, the fifth and sixth equalities follow.

Let us prove the commutation relations for long ribbons using the recursive formula of ribbon operators. Let
\rho_{A}=\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}
be a decomposition, where
\rho_{2}
is a short ribbon such that the relations (
102
) and (
104
) hold. Note that there is no overlap for
\rho_{1}
and
s_{b}=\partial_{1}\rho_{A}
. Then one computes

\displaystyle\quad A^{k}(s_{b})F^{[h],\varphi}(\rho_{A})

\displaystyle=\sum A^{k}(s_{b})F^{[h^{(1)}],\hat{\ell}}(\rho_{1})F^{[S(\ell^{(3)})h^{(2)}\ell^{(1)}],\varphi(\ell^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum F^{[h^{(1)}],\hat{\ell}}(\rho_{1})A^{k}(s_{b})F^{[S(\ell^{(3)})h^{(2)}\ell^{(1)}],\varphi(\ell^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum F^{[h^{(1)}],\hat{\ell}}(\rho_{1})F^{[S(\ell^{(3)})h^{(2)}\ell^{(1)}],\varphi(\ell^{(2)}\bullet k^{(2)})}(\rho_{2})A^{k^{(1)}}(s_{b})

\displaystyle=\sum F^{[h],\varphi(\bullet k^{(2)})}(\rho_{A})A^{k^{(1)}}(s_{b}).

Similarly, one has

\displaystyle\quad B^{\psi}(s_{b})F^{[h],\varphi}(\rho_{A})

\displaystyle=\sum B^{\psi}(s_{b})F^{[h^{(1)}],\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{[S(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)}],\varphi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum F^{[h^{(1)}],\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})B^{\psi}(s_{b})F^{[S(k^{(3)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)}],\varphi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi(k^{(3)}j^{(2)})F^{[h^{(1)}],\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{[S(k^{(5)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)}],\hat{j}}(\rho_{2})B^{\psi(S(j^{(3)})S(k^{(4)})h^{(3)}k^{(2)}j^{(1)}\bullet)}(s_{b})

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi(\ell^{(2)})F^{[h^{(1)}],\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{[S(k^{(5)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)}],\hat{\ell}(k^{(2)}j)\hat{j}}(\rho_{2})B^{\psi(S(\ell^{(3)})h^{(3)}\ell^{(1)}\bullet)}(s_{b})

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi(\ell^{(2)})F^{[h^{(1)}],\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{[S(k^{(5)})h^{(2)}k^{(1)}],\hat{\ell}(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2})B^{\psi(S(\ell^{(3)})h^{(3)}\ell^{(1)}\bullet)}(s_{b})

\displaystyle=\sum\varphi(\ell^{(2)})F^{[h^{(1)}],\hat{\ell}}(\rho)B^{\psi(S(\ell^{(3)})h^{(3)}\ell^{(1)}\bullet)}(s_{b}).

Here
\{k\}
,
\{j\}
and
\{\ell\}
are bases of
K
. We used
k^{(2)}j=\sum_{\ell}\hat{\ell}(k^{(2)}j)\ell
in the fourth equality, and used
\bullet=\hat{j}(\bullet)j
in the fifth equality.

Appendix D
Gapped boundary lattice based on generalized quantum double

Let us now introduce a general construction of a gapped boundary model based on a generalized quantum double.
The cellulation of the surface for this construction is slightly different from the one given above.
As shown in Fig.
5
, the boundary is drawn as a bold solid line. The boundary vertices are vertices on the line, which we denote as
v_{b}
. The boundary face is the right end of the dual boundary edges. There is only one boundary face for each boundary and we denote it as
f_{b}
. There are two kinds of boundary sites: the one
s_{b}=(v_{b},f_{b})
consists of boundary vertex and boundary face, which we call the outer boundary site; the one
s_{b}=(v_{b},f)
consists of boundary vertex and inner face, which we call inner boundary site.

Definition 25
.

A pairing
\lambda=\langle\bullet,\bullet\rangle:J\otimes K\to\mathbb{C}
between two Hopf algebras
J,K
is a bilinear map satisfying

\displaystyle\langle hg,a\rangle=\sum_{(a)}\langle h,a^{(1)}\rangle\langle g,a^{(2)}\rangle,

(204)

\displaystyle\langle h,ab\rangle=\sum_{(h)}\langle h^{(1)},a\rangle\langle h^{(2)},b\rangle,

(205)

\displaystyle\langle 1_{J},a\rangle=\varepsilon_{K}(a),\quad\langle h,1_{K}\rangle=\varepsilon_{J}(h).

(206)

There is a convolution algebra structure over
C:=\mathrm{Hom}(J\otimes K,\mathbb{C})
, with the convolution defined as

\begin{split}(\varphi*\psi)(h\otimes a):&=(\mu_{\mathbb{C}}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}(\varphi\otimes\psi)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\Delta_{J\otimes K})(h\otimes a)\\
&=\sum_{(h),(a)}\varphi(h^{(1)}\otimes a^{(1)})\psi(h^{(2)}\otimes a^{(2)}).\end{split}

(207)

The multiplication and unit of
C
are defined as

\displaystyle\mu_{C}(\varphi\otimes\psi)=\varphi*\psi,\quad\eta_{C}=\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\varepsilon_{J\otimes K}=\varepsilon_{J}\varepsilon_{K}.

(208)

It is easy to verify that (for semisimple Hopf algebras), the convolutional inverse of
\lambda
is

\lambda^{-1}(h\otimes a)=\langle S(h),a\rangle=\langle h,S(a)\rangle=\langle S^{-1}(h),a\rangle=\langle h,S^{-1}(a)\rangle.

(209)

Hereinafter, we assume that the pairing is also consistent with the
C^{*}
structure in the sense that
\lambda(\varphi^{*}\otimes h)=\overline{\lambda(\varphi\otimes S(h)^{*})}
.

Definition 26

(Generalized quantum double
Majid 2000
)
.

For a given pairing
\lambda:J\otimes K\to\mathbb{C}
, the generalized quantum double
D_{\lambda}(J^{\rm cop},K):=J^{\rm cop}\Join_{\lambda}K
is a Hopf algebra built on
J^{\rm cop}\otimes K
with Hopf algebra multiplication given by

(h\otimes a)(g\otimes b)=\sum_{(a),(g)}hg^{(2)}\otimes a^{(2)}b\lambda(g^{(1)}\otimes a^{(1)})\lambda^{-1}(g^{(3)}\otimes a^{(3)}),

(210)

where the comultiplication of
g
is taken in
J^{\rm cop}
.

When taking
J=\hat{H}
,
K=H
and
\lambda(\varphi\otimes x)=\varphi(x)
, one recovers the Drinfeld quantum double
D(H)=\hat{H}^{\rm cop}\Join H
as shown in Appendix
A
.

To construct the topological boundary of the Hopf quantum double model with the bulk determined by a Hopf algebra
H
, we need to impose some conditions on
J,K
such that they are related to
H
.
Here we will assume that there exists a left
H
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
such that the category of representations of
J^{\rm cop}\Join_{\lambda}K
is equivalent to the category of
H
-covariant
\mathfrak{A}|\mathfrak{A}
-bimodules, that is,
\mathsf{Rep}(J^{\rm cop}\Join_{\lambda}K)\simeq{{}_{\mathfrak{A}}^{H}}\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{A}}
.
The physical meaning of this restriction has been illustrated in Sec.
3.2
.
If
\mathfrak{A}
is a left
H
-comodule algebra with coaction
\beta_{\mathfrak{A}}
, by
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}
we mean the right
H
-comodule structure with opposite underlying algebra
\mathfrak{A}^{\rm op}
and coaction
\bar{\beta}_{\bar{\mathfrak{A}}}(x)=\sum_{(x)}x^{[0]}\otimes S^{-1}(x^{[-1]})
(where
\beta_{\mathfrak{A}}(x)=\sum_{(x)}x^{[-1]}\otimes x^{[0]}
).
If
\mathfrak{A}
is a right
H
-comodule algebra, similarly,
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}
is a left
H
-comodule algebra.
For a right
H
-comodule
(M,\beta_{M})
and a left
H
-comodule
(N,\beta_{N})
, the cotensor product
M\Box_{H}N
is the equalizer of the morphisms
\beta_{M}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{N}
and
\mathrm{id}_{M}\otimes\beta_{N}
, in the sense as follows:

M\Box_{H}N=\left\{\sum_{i}m_{i}\otimes n_{i}~\bigg|~\sum_{i}\beta_{M}(m_{i})\otimes n_{i}=\sum_{i}m_{i}\otimes\beta_{N}(n_{i})\right\}.

(211)

When
\mathfrak{A}
is a Galois-Hopf extension, the representation category is equivalent to the left module category over
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}\Box_{H}\mathfrak{A}
. This will be helpful for constructing boundary ribbon operators.
As we will see later, the above restriction for
J,K
is equivalent to that the representation category of the generalized quantum double is equivalent to the
H
-covariant
\mathfrak{A}|\mathfrak{A}
-bimodule category.
We would like to stress that this restriction is not artificial, since for any such
J^{\rm cop}\Join_{\lambda}K
, starting from the representation category
\mathsf{Rep}(J^{\rm cop}\Join_{\lambda}K)
, we can construct such an
H
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{A}
which satisfies the above assumption. Details have been given Sec.
3.2
.

Figure 5:
The depiction of the boundary, boundary face, boundary vertex, and boundary site.

Now we are ready to present the construction of the boundary Hamiltonian. The boundary is characterized by the following collection of data: the bulk Hopf algebra
H
and the boundary Hopf algebras
J,K,W
. They satisfy the following conditions:

•

Two finite-dimensional
C^{*}
Hopf algebras
K
and
J
for which there exists a pairing
\lambda:J\otimes K\to\mathbb{C}
. And there is a pairing between
\hat{H}
and
K
,
\gamma:\hat{H}\otimes K\to\mathbb{C}
.

•

A finite-dimensional
C^{*}
Hopf algebra
W
, which is a
K
-bimodule coalgebra.
The left and right
K
-actions are denoted as
h\triangleright w
and
w\triangleleft h
respectively for
w\in W,~h\in K
. The right action induces a left
K
-module structure by
w\triangleleft S(h)
. We denote the operators corresponding to these two actions as

L_{+}^{K,h}w=h\triangleright w=hw,\quad L_{-}^{K,h}w=w\triangleleft S(h)=wS(h).

(212)

We also assume that the antipode law holds:
S(h\triangleright w)=S(w)\triangleleft S(h)
and
S(w\triangleleft h)=S(h)\triangleright S(w)
;
for
C^{*}
structure, we assume
(h\triangleright w)^{*}=w^{*}\triangleleft h^{*}
and
(w\triangleleft h)^{*}=h^{*}\triangleright w^{*}
; and for Haar integral
h_{W}\in W
and
k\in K
, we assume
kh_{W}=\varepsilon(k)h_{W}=h_{W}k
.

•

H
is a
K
-bimodule coalgebra that satisfies the antipode law.

•

There is a pairing
\zeta:J\otimes W\to\mathbb{C}
between
J
and
W
.
The pairing induces the
J
-bimodule structures of
W
: left module
\varphi\rightharpoonup w=\sum_{(w)}w^{(1)}\zeta(\varphi\otimes w^{(2)})
, and right module
w\leftharpoonup\varphi=\sum_{(w)}w^{(2)}\zeta(\varphi\otimes w^{(1)})
. The right module structure induces a left module structure by
w\leftharpoonup S(\varphi)
.
The corresponding two left module structure operators are denoted as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} T^{J,\varphi}_{+}&w=\varphi\rightharpoonup w=\sum_{(w)}w^{(1)}\zeta(\varphi\otimes w^{(2)}),\\
T^{J,\varphi}_{-}w&=w\leftharpoonup S(\varphi)=\sum_{(w)}w^{(2)}\zeta^{-1}(\varphi\otimes w^{(1)}).\end{aligned}

(213)

For the Haar integral
\varphi_{\hat{W}}
, we assume that
\sum_{(x)}\langle\varphi_{\hat{W}},x^{(1)}\rangle\zeta(\psi,x^{(2)})=\varepsilon_{J}(\psi)\langle\varphi_{\hat{W}},x\rangle
for all
\psi\in J
and
x\in W
.

•

There is a pairing
\alpha:\hat{H}\otimes W\to\mathbb{C}
which also induces an
\hat{H}
-bimodule structure over
W
in a similar way as in Eq. (
213
).
It is easy to verify that
W
is also an
\hat{H}
-module algebra, and this module algebra structure induces an
H
-comodule algebra over
W
.
For the Haar integral
\varphi_{\hat{W}}
, we assume that
\sum_{(x)}\langle\varphi_{\hat{W}},x^{(1)}\rangle\alpha(\psi,x^{(2)})=\varepsilon_{\hat{H}}(\psi)\langle\varphi_{\hat{W}},x\rangle
for all
\psi\in\hat{H}
and
x\in W
.

•

Different pairings are consistent when acting on two Hopf algebras for which there is a module structure. For example,
\lambda:J\otimes K\to\mathbb{C}
and
\zeta:J\otimes W\to\mathbb{C}
are two pairings and
W
has left
K
-module structures
h\triangleright w
and
w\triangleleft S(h)
, then the consistency conditions read

\displaystyle\sum_{(\varphi)}\lambda(\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h)\zeta(\varphi^{(2)}\otimes w)

\displaystyle=\zeta(\varphi\otimes(h\triangleright w)),

(214)

\displaystyle\sum_{(\varphi)}\lambda(\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h)\zeta^{-1}(\varphi^{(2)}\otimes w)

\displaystyle=\zeta(\varphi\otimes(w\triangleleft S(h))).

(215)

Remark D.1
.

As in the bulk, we can also construct the boundary using the right-module structure; the generalization is straightforward.

For each boundary edge
e_{b}
, we assign a Hopf algebra
\mathcal{H}_{e_{b}}=W
. The total boundary space becomes
\mathcal{H}(\partial\Sigma)=\otimes_{e_{b}\in E(\partial\Sigma)}\mathcal{H}_{e_{b}}
, and therefore, the total Hilbert space becomes
\mathcal{H}_{tot}=\mathcal{H}(\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma)\otimes\mathcal{H}(\partial\Sigma)
.
As shown in Fig.
5
, the boundary corresponds to the shaded region, and there is only one boundary face outside a given boundary.
For boundary site
s_{b}=(v_{b},f_{b})
and
h\in K
, we can define (in counterclockwise order),

A^{h}(s_{b})=\sum_{(h)}L^{K,h^{(1)}}(j_{1},v_{b})\otimes\cdots\otimes L^{K,h^{(n)}}(j_{n},v_{b}),

(216)

where, for bulk edges,
L^{K,h^{(\ell)}}(j_{\ell},v_{b})
act on
H
, and for boundary edges, act on
W
. The convention to choose
L_{+},L_{-}
is the same as in the bulk.

For the outer boundary site
s_{b}
and
\varphi\in J
, we introduce an operator which acts non-trivially only on all boundary edges (in counterclockwise order),

B^{\varphi}(s_{b})=\sum_{(\varphi)}T^{J,\varphi^{(1)}}(j_{1},f_{b})\otimes T^{J,\varphi^{(2)}}(j_{2},f_{b})\otimes\cdots\otimes T^{J,\varphi^{(n)}}(j_{n},f_{b}).

(217)

The convention to choose
T_{+},T_{-}
is also the same as in the bulk. For example,

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{aligned}\quad\begin{aligned} &B^{\varphi}(s_{b})|w_{1},\cdots,w_{n}\rangle\\
=&\sum_{(w_{1}),\cdots,(w_{n}),(\varphi)}\zeta(\varphi^{(1)}\otimes w_{1}^{(2)})\cdots\zeta(\varphi^{(n)}\otimes w_{n}^{(2)})|w_{1}^{(1)},\cdots,w_{n}^{(1)}\rangle\\
=&\sum_{(w_{1}),\cdots,(w_{n})}\zeta(\varphi\otimes w_{1}^{(2)}\cdots w_{n}^{(2)})|w_{1}^{(1)},\cdots,w_{n}^{(1)}\rangle,\end{aligned}

(218)

where comultiplication components
\varphi^{(i)}
are taken in
J
.

Proposition 13
.

For the outer boundary site
s_{b}
, the operators
A^{h}(s_{b})
and
B^{\varphi}(s_{b})
satisfy

A^{h}(s_{b})B^{\varphi}(s_{b})=\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}\lambda(\varphi^{(3)}\otimes h^{(1)})\lambda^{-1}(\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(3)})B^{\varphi^{(2)}}(s_{b})A^{h^{(2)}}(s_{b}).

(219)

Therefore, by mapping
\varphi\otimes h\in J^{\rm cop}\Join_{\lambda}K
to
B^{\varphi}(s_{b})A^{h}(s_{b})
, we obtain a representation of
J^{\rm cop}\Join_{\lambda}K
over
\mathcal{H}(s_{b})=\otimes_{e\in\partial s_{b}}\mathcal{H}_{e}
.

Proof.

Consider the setting as in Eq. (
218
), we see that

\displaystyle A^{h}(s_{b})B^{\varphi}(s_{b})|w_{1},\cdots,w_{n}\rangle|x_{1}\rangle

(220)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(w_{i}),(h)}\zeta(\varphi\otimes w_{1}^{(2)}\cdots w_{n}^{(2)})|h^{(3)}w_{1}^{(1)},\cdots,w_{n}^{(1)}S(h^{(1)})\rangle|h^{(2)}x_{1}\rangle.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}\lambda(\varphi^{(3)}\otimes h^{(1)})\lambda^{-1}(\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(3)})B^{\varphi^{(2)}}(s_{b})A^{h^{(2)}}(s_{b})|w_{1},\cdots,w_{n}\rangle|x_{1}\rangle

(221)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(w_{i}),(h),(\varphi)}\lambda(\varphi^{(3)}\otimes h^{(1)})\lambda^{-1}(\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(5)})B^{\varphi^{(2)}}(s_{b})|h^{(4)}w_{1},\cdots,w_{n}S(h^{(2)})\rangle|h^{(3)}x_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(w_{i}),(h),(\varphi)}\lambda(\varphi^{(3)}\otimes h^{(1)})\lambda^{-1}(\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(5)})\zeta(\varphi^{(2)}\otimes(h^{(4)}w_{1})^{(2)}w_{2}^{(2)}\cdots(w_{n}S(h^{(2)}))^{(2)})

\displaystyle|(h^{(4)}w_{1})^{(1)},w_{2}^{(1)},\cdots,(w_{n}S(h^{(2)}))^{(1)})\rangle|h^{(3)}x_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(w_{i}),(h)}\zeta(\varphi\otimes S^{-1}(h^{(7)})h^{(6)}w_{1}^{(2)}w_{2}^{(2)}\cdots w_{n}^{(2)}S(h^{(2)})h^{(1)})

\displaystyle|h^{(5)}w_{1}^{(1)},w_{2}^{(1)},\cdots,w_{n}^{(1)}S(h^{(3)})\rangle|h^{(4)}x_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(w_{i}),(h)}\zeta(\varphi\otimes w_{1}^{(2)}\cdots w_{n}^{(2)})|h^{(3)}w_{1}^{(1)},\cdots,w_{n}^{(1)}S(h^{(1)})\rangle|h^{(2)}x_{1}\rangle.

This completes the proof of Eq. (
219
).
Using Eq. (
219
), it is easy to check that
B^{\varphi}(s_{b})A^{h}(s_{b})
is a representation of
J^{\rm cop}\Join K
.
∎

Since
K
and
J
are both
C^{*}
Hopf algebras, they both have unique Haar integrals
h_{K}
and
\varphi_{J}
. Then we can define the boundary stabilizer operators as

A^{K}_{v_{b}}=A^{h_{K}}(s_{b}),\quad B^{J}_{f_{b}}=B^{\varphi_{J}}(s_{b}).

(222)

Since both of
h_{K}
and
\varphi_{J}
are cocommutative, the vertex and face operators only depend on their respective vertices and faces.

Proposition 14
.

All boundary stabilizer operators are projectors, and satisfy

\displaystyle(A^{h_{K}}_{v_{b}})^{\dagger}=A^{h_{K}^{*}}_{v_{b}}=A^{h_{K}}_{v_{b}},\quad(A^{h_{K}}_{v_{b}})^{2}=A^{h_{K}^{2}}_{v_{b}}=A^{h_{K}}_{v_{b}},

(223)

\displaystyle(B^{\varphi_{J}}_{f_{b}})^{\dagger}=B^{\varphi_{J}^{*}}_{f_{b}}=B^{\varphi_{J}}_{f_{b}},\quad(B^{\varphi_{J}}_{f_{b}})^{2}=B^{\varphi_{J}^{2}}_{f_{b}}=B^{\varphi_{J}}_{f_{b}}.

(224)

All boundary stabilizer operators commute with each other and with all bulk stabilizer operators.

Proof.

The inner product of
W
is determined by the Haar integral
\varphi_{\hat{W}}
, i.e.,
\langle x,y\rangle:=\varphi_{\hat{W}}(x^{*}y)
.
We see that
\langle x,L_{+}^{h}y\rangle=\varphi_{\hat{W}}(x^{*}(h\triangleright y))=\varphi_{\hat{W}}((h^{*}\triangleright x)^{*}y)=\langle L_{+}^{h^{*}}x,y\rangle
, thus
(L_{+}^{h})^{\dagger}=L_{+}^{h^{*}}
. Similarly,
(L^{h}_{-})^{\dagger}=L^{h^{*}}_{-}
.
This implies that
(A^{h_{K}}_{v_{b}})^{\dagger}=\sum_{(h_{K})}\otimes_{j}(L^{h^{(j)}_{K}}(j,v_{b}))^{\dagger}=\sum_{(h_{K})}\otimes_{j}L^{{h^{(j)}_{K}}^{*}}(j,v_{b})=A^{h_{K}^{*}}_{v_{b}}=A^{h_{K}}_{v_{b}}
.

Similarly, it follows that

\displaystyle\langle x,T^{\varphi}_{+}y\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y)}\varphi_{\hat{W}}(x^{*}y^{(1)}\zeta(\varphi\otimes y^{(2)}))

(225)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y),(x^{*}),(\varphi)}\varphi_{\hat{W}}({x^{*}}^{(1)}\zeta(1_{J}\otimes{x^{*}}^{(2)})\varepsilon_{J}(\varphi^{(2)})\zeta(\varphi^{(1)}\otimes y^{(2)})y^{(1)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y),(x^{*}),(\varphi)}\varphi_{\hat{W}}({x^{*}}^{(1)}\zeta(\varphi^{(2)}S(\varphi^{(3)})\otimes{x^{*}}^{(2)})\zeta(\varphi^{(1)}\otimes y^{(2)})y^{(1)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y),(x^{*}),(\varphi)}\varphi_{\hat{W}}({x^{*}}^{(1)}y^{(1)}\zeta(\varphi^{(2)}\otimes{x^{*}}^{(2)})\zeta(S(\varphi^{(3)})\otimes{x^{*}}^{(3)})\zeta(\varphi^{(1)}\otimes y^{(2)}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y),(x^{*}),(\varphi)}\varphi_{\hat{W}}(y^{(1)}{x^{*}}^{(1)}\zeta(\varphi^{(1)}\otimes y^{(2)}{x^{*}}^{(2)})\zeta(S(\varphi^{(2)})\otimes{x^{*}}^{(3)}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x^{*}),(y),(\varphi)}\varphi_{\hat{W}}((y{x^{*}}^{(1)})^{(1)}\zeta(\varphi^{(1)}\otimes(y{x^{*}}^{(1)})^{(2)})\zeta(S(\varphi^{(2)})\otimes{x^{*}}^{(3)}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x^{*})}\varphi_{\hat{W}}({x^{*}}^{(1)}y\zeta(S(\varphi)\otimes{x^{*}}^{(2)}))=\langle T_{+}^{\varphi^{*}}x,y\rangle.

This means that
(T^{\varphi}_{+})^{\dagger}=T^{\varphi^{*}}_{+}
. We can similarly show that
(T^{\varphi}_{-})^{\dagger}=T^{\varphi^{*}}_{-}
.
Thus we see that
(B^{\varphi_{J}}_{f_{b}})^{\dagger}=B^{\varphi_{J}^{*}}_{f_{b}}=B^{\varphi_{J}}_{f_{b}}
.

Using Proposition
13
and the fact that
h_{K}^{2}=h_{K}
and
\varphi_{J}^{2}=\varphi_{J}
, we see that
A^{h_{K}}_{v_{b}}
and
B_{f_{b}}^{\varphi_{J}}
are idempotent.
Further using the fact that
h_{K}
and
\varphi_{J}
are cocommutative, we can show that all stabilizers are commutative.
∎

The boundary Hamiltonian is given by the local commuting projector Hamiltonian

H_{K,{J}}(\partial\Sigma)=\sum_{v_{b}\in V(\partial\Sigma)}(I-A_{v_{b}}^{K})+\sum_{f_{b}\in F(\partial\Sigma)}(I-B^{J}_{f_{b}}).

(226)

The total Hamiltonian is thus

H_{H}^{K,J}=H_{H}(\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma)+H_{K,J}(\partial\Sigma).

(227)

This model will be called the extended Hopf algebraic quantum double model.

The ground state degeneracy now depends on both the topology of the
2d
surface
\Sigma
and its boundaries. It is still independent of the choice of the cellulation
C(\Sigma)
.

\operatorname{GSD}=\operatorname{Tr}(\prod_{v\in V(\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma)}A^{H}_{v}\prod_{f\in F(\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma)}B_{f}^{\hat{H}}\prod_{v_{b}\in V(\partial\Sigma)}A^{K}_{v_{b}}\prod_{f_{b}\in F(\partial\Sigma)}B_{f_{b}}^{{J}})

(228)

When
\Sigma
is a sphere, by cutting boundaries, the ground state space will become degenerate.

To shed further light on this construction of boundaries, let us consider two examples.

Example 27
.

Consider the case that
W=H
,
J\leq\hat{H}
and
K\leq H
are Hopf subalgebras. We see that they satisfy all the requirements for constructing the boundary. In this kind of model, it is more convenient to discuss boundary condensation and confinement using the ribbon operators.

Example 28
.

For finite group algebra
H=\mathbb{C}[G]
,
take
W=\mathbb{C}[G]
,
K=\mathbb{C}[M]
,
J=\mathbb{C}^{G/N}
with
M
a subgroup and
N
a normal subgroup of
G
. We can also obtain a corresponding generalized model.
This model is similar to the one given in
Bombin and Martin-Delgado 2008
.

Ribbon operator.
—
Let us briefly discuss the construction of ribbon operators. Assume that
J\leq\hat{H}
and
K\leq H
are subalgebras. The comultiplication of the dual
D_{\lambda}(J^{\rm cop},K)^{\vee}\cong\hat{J}\otimes\hat{K}
is given by

\Delta(h\otimes\varphi)=\sum_{k,j}\sum_{(k),(j),(h)}(h^{(1)}\otimes\hat{k})\otimes(\hat{j}\otimes\varphi(k^{(2)}\bullet))\lambda(j^{(3)}\otimes k^{(1)})\lambda^{-1}(j^{(1)}\otimes k^{(3)})h^{(2)}(j^{(2)}),

for
h\in\hat{J}
and
\varphi\in\hat{K}
, where
\{k\}\subset K
and
\{j\}\subset J
are bases. Define the operators
L^{\varphi}_{\pm},\,T^{h}_{\pm}:W\to W
by

L^{\hat{k}}_{\pm}:=L^{K,k}_{\pm},\quad T^{\hat{j}}_{\pm}:=T^{J,j}_{\pm},

on bases and then by linearity. Since
J\subset\hat{H}
and
K\subset H
, one can define the operators
L^{\varphi}_{\pm},\,T^{h}_{\pm}:H\to H
by

L^{\hat{k}}_{\pm}:=L^{k}_{\pm},\quad T^{\hat{j}}_{\pm}:=T^{j}_{\pm},

on bases and then by linearity. One can also defines
\tilde{L}^{\varphi}_{\pm},\tilde{T}^{h}_{\pm}
using the right module structures in Remark
D.1
.

Having defined these operators, we can define the triangle operators similar to Eqs. (
17
)-(
24
). For a bulk-to-boundary ribbon
\rho=\rho_{\downarrow}=\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}
, the ribbon operator
F^{h,\varphi}(\rho)
for
h\in\hat{J}
and
\varphi\in\hat{K}
is defined by

F^{h,\varphi}(\rho)=\sum_{k,j}\sum_{(k),(j),(h)}\lambda(j^{(3)}\otimes k^{(1)})\lambda^{-1}(j^{(1)}\otimes k^{(3)})h^{(2)}(j^{(2)})F^{h^{(1)},\hat{k}}(\rho_{1})F^{\hat{j},\varphi(k^{(2)}\bullet)}(\rho_{2}).

This is independent of the choice of the decomposition
\rho=\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}
.
One can compute the commutation relations of the local operators and the ribbon operators with the same strategy as we have discussed before.

Appendix E
Domain wall lattice based on generalized quantum double

Suppose that two
2d
quantum double phases, which are respectively characterized by Hopf algebras
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
, are separated by a
1d
domain wall, see Fig.
4
(a).
To construct the domain wall Hamiltonian, we need the following input data (all Hopf algebras here are finite-dimensional
C^{*}
Hopf algebras):

•

Two domain wall face Hopf algebras
J_{1}
,
J_{2}
and one domain wall vertex Hopf algebra
K
, and there exist pairings,
\lambda_{1}:J_{1}\otimes K\to\mathbb{C}
and
\lambda_{2}:J_{2}\otimes K\to\mathbb{C}
.

•

A domain wall Hopf algebra
W
which is a
K
-bimodule. The left actions of
K
on
W
induced by
h\triangleright w
and
w\triangleleft S(h)
are denoted as
L^{K,h}_{+}
and
L_{-}^{K,h}
respectively.

•

Two bulk Hopf algebras
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
which are
K
-bimodules. The bimodule structures also induce two inequivalent left module structures on
H_{i}
.

•

There are pairings
\zeta_{1}:J_{1}\otimes W\to\mathbb{C}
and
\zeta_{2}:J_{2}\otimes W\to\mathbb{C}
.
These two pairings induce the
J_{1}
- and
J_{2}
-bimodule structures on
W
:
\phi\rightharpoonup w=\sum_{(w)}w^{(1)}\zeta_{i}(\phi\otimes w^{(2)})
and
w\leftharpoonup\phi=\sum_{(w)}w^{(2)}\zeta_{i}(\phi\otimes w^{(1)})
.
This further induces two left
J_{i}
-module structure operators

T^{J_{i},\phi}_{+}w=\phi\rightharpoonup w,\quad T^{J_{i},\phi}_{-}w=w\leftharpoonup S(\phi).

(229)

•

There are pairings
\xi_{1}:J_{1}\otimes H_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
and
\xi_{2}:J_{2}\otimes H_{2}\to\mathbb{C}
, which also induce two inequivalent left
J_{i}
-module structures on
H_{i}
.

Similar to the gapped boundary construction, we will also assume some consistency conditions for these pairings and actions.

For each edge in the first and second bulks
C(\Sigma_{1})
and
C(\Sigma_{2})
, we assign the Hopf algebras
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
respectively. Two bulk total Hilbert spaces are thus
\mathcal{H}(\Sigma_{i})=\otimes_{e\in C(\Sigma_{i})}H_{i}
with
i=1,2
.
For each domain wall edge
e_{d}\in C(\Sigma_{d})
(where
\Sigma_{d}=\partial\Sigma_{1}=\partial\Sigma_{2}
), we attach the Hopf algebra
W
to it.
The total domain wall Hilbert space is
\mathcal{H}(\Sigma_{d})=\otimes_{e_{d}\in C(\Sigma_{d})}W
.
We define the domain wall vertex as the vertex on the wall and denote them as
v_{d}
. The domain wall face on the first bulk is the face near the wall in the first bulk, and it is denoted as
f_{d,1}
. Similarly, we denote
f_{d,2}
for the domain wall face on the second bulk.

For a domain wall site
s_{d}=(v_{d},f_{d})
and
h\in K
, we defined (in counterclockwise order)

A^{h}(s_{d})=\sum_{(h)}L^{K,h^{(1)}}(j_{1},v_{d})\otimes\cdots\otimes L^{K,h^{(n)}}(j_{n},v_{d}),

(230)

where, for first and second bulk edges,
L^{K,h^{(\ell)}}(j_{\ell},v_{d})
act on
H_{i}
, and for domain wall edges, they act on
W
. The convention for choosing
L_{+},L_{-}
is the same as in the bulk.

For the domain wall site
s_{d,i}
on the
i
-th bulk and
\varphi\in J_{i}^{\rm cop}
, we defined the face operator (in counterclockwise order)

B^{\varphi}(s_{d,i})=\sum_{(\varphi)}T^{J_{i},\varphi^{(1)}}(j_{1},f_{d,i})\otimes T^{J_{i},\varphi^{(2)}}(j_{2},f_{d,i})\otimes\cdots\otimes T^{J_{i},\varphi^{(n)}}(j_{n},f_{d,i}),

(231)

where the comultiplication is taken in
J_{i}
. The convention for choosing
T_{+},T_{-}
is the same as in the bulk.

Proposition 15
.

For the domain wall site
s_{d,i}
, the operators
A^{h}(s_{d,i})
and
B^{\varphi}(s_{d,i})
satisfy

A^{h}(s_{d,i})B^{\varphi}(s_{d,i})=\sum_{(h),(\varphi)}\lambda_{i}(\varphi^{(3)}\otimes h^{(1)})\lambda_{i}^{-1}(\varphi^{(1)}\otimes h^{(3)})B^{\varphi^{(2)}}(s_{d,i})A^{h^{(2)}}(s_{d,i}).

(232)

Therefore, by mapping
\varphi\otimes h\in J_{i}^{\rm cop}\Join_{\lambda_{i}}K
to
B^{\varphi}(s_{d,i})A^{h}(s_{d,i})
, we obtain a representation of
J^{\rm cop}_{i}\Join_{\lambda_{i}}K
over
\mathcal{H}_{i}(s_{d,i})=\otimes_{e\in\partial s_{d,i}}\mathcal{H}_{e}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one in Proposition
13
, so we omit it here.
∎

Now by choosing the Haar integrals
h_{K}\in K
and
\varphi_{J_{i}}\in J_{i}
, we define domain wall stabilizers as

A^{K}_{v_{d}}=A^{h_{K}}(s_{d}),\quad B^{J_{i}}_{f_{d,i}}=B^{\varphi_{J_{i}}}(s_{d,i}).

(233)

All domain wall stabilizer operators are projectors and satisfy

\displaystyle(A^{h_{K}}_{v_{b}})^{\dagger}=A^{h_{K}^{*}}_{v_{b}}

\displaystyle=A^{h_{K}}_{v_{b}},\quad(A^{h_{K}}_{v_{b}})^{2}=A^{h_{K}^{2}}=A^{h_{K}}_{v_{b}},

(234)

\displaystyle(B^{\varphi_{J_{i}}}_{f_{d,i}})^{\dagger}=B^{\varphi_{J_{i}}^{*}}_{f_{d,i}}

\displaystyle=B^{\varphi_{J_{i}}}_{f_{d,i}},\quad(B^{\varphi_{J_{i}}}_{f_{d,i}})^{2}=B^{\varphi_{J_{i}}^{2}}_{f_{d,i}}=B^{\varphi_{J_{i}}}_{f_{d,i}}.

(235)

All boundary stabilizer operators commute with each other and with all bulk stabilizer operators.
The domain wall Hamiltonian can be constructed as

H^{K,J_{1},J_{2}}(\Sigma_{d})=\sum_{v_{d}}(I-A^{K}_{v_{d}})+\sum_{f_{d,1}}(I-B^{J_{1}}_{f_{d,1}})+\sum_{f_{d,2}}(I-B^{J_{2}}_{f_{d,2}}).

(236)

The total Hamiltonian is thus

H_{H_{1},H_{2}}^{K,J_{1},J_{2}}=H_{H_{1}}(\Sigma_{1})+H_{H_{2}}(\Sigma_{2})+H^{K,J_{1},J_{2}}(\Sigma_{d}).

(237)

Example 29
.

To take a closer look at the construction, let us consider a simple example.
Choose
J_{1}\subseteq\hat{H}_{1}
,
J_{2}\subseteq\hat{H}_{2}
and
W=H_{1}\otimes H_{2}
.
The pairings are set as

\displaystyle\langle\varphi_{1},h_{1}\otimes h_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle:=\langle\varphi_{1},h_{1}\rangle\langle\varepsilon,h_{2}\rangle=\varphi_{1}(h_{1})\varepsilon(h_{2}),

(238)

\displaystyle\langle\varphi_{2},h_{1}\otimes h_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle:=\langle\varepsilon,h_{1}\rangle\langle\varphi_{2},h_{2}\rangle=\varepsilon(h_{1})\varphi_{2}(h_{2}),

(239)

for
\varphi_{i}\in J_{i}
. It is easy to verify that these give well-defined pairings.
For the edge operators induced by these pairings, we have

\displaystyle T^{J_{1},\varphi}_{\pm}h_{1}\otimes h_{2}=(T^{J_{1},\varphi}_{\pm}h_{1})\otimes h_{2},

(240)

\displaystyle T^{J_{2},\varphi}_{\pm}h_{1}\otimes h_{2}=h_{1}\otimes(T^{J_{2},\varphi}_{\pm}h_{2}).

(241)

The action decouples from the tensor product of two phases.
We could introduce a domain wall Hopf algebra
K
to sew them together. The actions of
K
on
H_{i}
naturally induce an action of
K
on
W=H_{1}\otimes H_{2}
as
k\triangleright(h_{1}\otimes h_{2})=\sum_{(k)}k^{(1)}h_{1}\otimes k^{(2)}h_{2}
. To simplify the notation, we will denote the action of
K
on
H_{i}
as
kh
and
h_{i}S(k)
.
For example

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{aligned}\quad\begin{aligned} &A^{k}|h_{1}\otimes g_{1},h_{2},h_{3}\otimes g_{2},g_{3}\rangle\\
=&\sum_{(k)}|h_{2}S(k^{(1)}),k^{(2)}(h_{3}\otimes g_{2}),k^{(3)}g_{3},(h_{1}\otimes g_{1})S(k^{(4)})\rangle.\end{aligned}

(242)

We see that the gapped domain wall is determined by
K,J_{1},J_{2}
.

N
-bulk domain wall and folding trick.
—
The above discussion about the domain wall between two bulks can be generalized to the
N
-bulk scenario.
This
N
-bulk domain wall is crucial for investigating the higher-dimensional Hopf quantum double model.

In this
N
-bulk case, it turns out that we need extra input data, that is the direction for each bulk (denoted as
\sigma(i)
).
If we fix the direction of the domain wall, then using the right-hand screw rule, there is an induced direction
\tilde{\sigma}(i)
for each bulk.
This induced direction may or may not coincide with the direction of that bulk.
We assign a Hopf algebra
H_{i}
(equivalently, a UFC
\mathcal{C}_{i}=\mathsf{Rep}(H_{i})
) for each bulk.
An
N
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{B}
can be defined as follows: for arbitrary pair of bulks
H_{i},H_{j}
, if
\sigma(i)=\tilde{\sigma}(i)
and
\sigma(j)\neq\tilde{\sigma}(j)
, there is an
H_{1}\otimes H_{2}^{\rm cop}
-comodule structure over
\mathfrak{B}
, and all other situations can be treated similarly.
The
N
-bulk domain wall is thus characterized by this
N
-comodule algebra
\mathfrak{B}
.

The lattice construction is similar to the two-bulk case: we assign a Hopf algebra
W
for each wall edge; a Hopf algebra
K
for each wall vertex; and a Hopf algebra
J_{i}
for boundary face in the
H_{i}
-bulk.
They satisfy similar compatibility conditions to the two-bulk case.
The wall Hamiltonian becomes

H_{H_{1},\cdots,H_{N}}^{K,J_{1},\cdots,J_{N}}(\Sigma_{d})=\sum_{v_{d}}(I-A_{v_{d}}^{K})+\sum_{i}\sum_{f_{d,i}}(I-B^{J_{i}}_{f_{d,i}}).

(243)

The wall excitations are given by the
N
-module functor category
\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}|\cdots|\mathcal{C}_{N}}({{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod},{{}_{\mathfrak{B}}}\mathsf{Mod})
.
The folding trick also holds in this case, namely, by stacking all bulk together, the domain wall will become a gapped boundary.
